Looksmaxxing Trend Poses Health Risks for Young Men

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A clinical commentary published in the Journal of Adolescent Health warns that the viral online trend known as “looksmaxxing” exposes adolescent boys and young men to severe psychological and physical harms. Led by Dr. Kyle T. Ganson of the University of Toronto, the authors describe how attempts to optimize male facial and physical attractiveness cross from routine grooming into life-threatening behaviors, including extreme dietary restriction, unregulated peptide and steroid abuse, and self-injurious practices like “bonesmashing.”

The paper urges pediatricians, psychiatrists, and adolescent health clinicians to recognize looksmaxxing subcultures, differentiate benign hygiene from body dysmorphia, and screen for intersecting eating disorders, steroid use, and manosphere radicalization.

In recent years, the algorithmic landscape of social media platforms has given rise to “looksmaxxing”, an online subculture centered on the systematic pursuit of maximizing physical attractiveness. Rooted in anonymous web forums and popularized across TikTok, YouTube, and Discord, the trend presents male aesthetic value as an objective, quantifiable science governed by facial symmetry, prominent cheekbones, sharp mandibular angles, and muscular, low-body-fat physiques.

While early engagement may involve standard self-care, such as adopting a skincare regimen, lifting weights, or addressing androgenetic alopecia, the subculture frequently operates as an algorithmic pipeline toward radical body modification and psychological distress.

In a clinical commentary published in the Journal of Adolescent Health, adolescent health expert Dr. Kyle T. Ganson, PhD, MSW, associate professor at the University of Toronto’s Factor-Inwentash Faculty of Social Work, alongside international colleagues, issues a call to action for healthcare providers. The authors highlight that vulnerable boys and young men are increasingly turning to extreme, unregulated practices to conform to rigid physical archetypes, with severe repercussions for mental and physical health.

The researchers emphasize that looksmaxxing operates along a wide, insidious spectrum. At the lower-risk end, adolescents exchange tips on haircuts, dental hygiene, and fitness. However, the culture frequently pushes members toward “hardmaxxing”, invasive, unverified, or explicitly hazardous interventions.

“Looksmaxxing exists on a spectrum, and it is important that we do not automatically pathologize normal grooming, exercise, or appearance-related behaviours among adolescent boys and young adult men,” said Dr. Ganson. “At the same time, clinicians need to recognize when these behaviours become extreme, unsafe, or connected with significant distress and impairment.”

The rapid spread of looksmaxxing is accelerated by algorithmic echo chambers. Young men often place greater trust in charismatic social media influencers, manipulated “before-and-after” photographs, and anonymous numerical facial ratings than in qualified medical professionals.

Furthermore, looksmaxxing spaces rarely exist in a cultural vacuum. The commentary highlights how these forums frequently intersect with broader “manosphere” ideologies, incel (involuntary celibate) subcultures, and hyper-masculine content. In these spaces, physical appearance is treated as a deterministic prerequisite for romantic success, social status, and human worth. When young men inevitably fail to attain impossible physical ideals, the resulting cognitive distortions can lead to profound demoralization, severe depressive episodes, and suicidality.

Because traditional gender norms often discourage young men from admitting to body image insecurities or seeking psychiatric help, appearance-related distress in males frequently goes undetected until severe medical complications emerge.

The authors call on pediatricians, adolescent medicine specialists, dermatologists, and mental health clinicians to become fluent in looksmaxxing terminology and systematically screen young male patients presenting with obsessive fitness or grooming routines.

The commentary recommends comprehensive assessment across several key domains:

Rather than dismissing or ridiculing adolescents’ appearance concerns, which can alienate patients and push them deeper into insular online forums, the authors advocate for an empathetic, harm-reduction approach. Clinicians should validate young men’s desires for self-improvement while directly dismantling online pseudoscience, correcting biological misinformation, and warning against permanent structural injury.

Additionally, fostering digital media literacy can empower young men to deconstruct algorithmic manipulation, recognize edited imagery, and reject narrow, toxic beauty ideals.

“Boys and young men are navigating a digital environment where appearance advice, pseudoscience, commercial interests, and potentially dangerous health behaviours can become intertwined,” concluded Dr. Ganson. “Understanding that environment is increasingly important for clinicians, researchers, families, and others who support boys and young men.”

Looksmaxxing is an online subculture and trend popular among adolescent boys and young men dedicated to maximizing physical attractiveness. While it begins with common hygiene, grooming, and fitness practices, it often progresses to extreme, dangerous behaviors to achieve rigid aesthetic ideals like chiseled jawlines and hyper-muscular builds.

What are the most dangerous practices associated with looksmaxxing?

Extreme practices, often called “hardmaxxing”, include severe caloric starvation, the use of illicit anabolic steroids, peptides, and SARMs, unsupervised chemical skin whitening, and “bonesmashing” (striking one’s own facial bones with heavy objects in a misguided attempt to change jaw structure).

How should clinicians address looksmaxxing in adolescent male patients?

Clinicians should adopt a harm-reduction approach rather than dismissing appearance concerns. They should become familiar with looksmaxxing terminology, evaluate patients for body dysmorphic disorder, eating disorders, and steroid misuse, correct pseudoscientific misinformation, and address underlying loneliness, depression, or exposure to extremist online communities.

Looksmaxxing among adolescent boys and young men: increasing awareness among clinicians

Looksmaxxing is a contemporary phenomenon focused on “maximizing” physical attractiveness. Engagement with looksmaxxing exists along a spectrum, from seemingly benign grooming practices to dangerous and life-threatening behaviors; its underlying ideals are often rooted in pseudoscience, rigid appearance standards, and potentially harmful online community norms.

Clinicians must be prepared to recognize its language, assess the degree of involvement, and intervene with critical awareness and attention to co-occurring risks such as disordered eating, substance use, self-harm, and misogynistic or extremist beliefs.

Earth's churning mantle may have created the Great Unconformity

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Explaining the Great Unconformity, where Cambrian deposits sit on top of older rocks.

In the Grand Canyon, there is a line in the rock where time seems to have gone missing. Ancient crystalline rocks mark its bottom, while sandstones laid down as life started to diversify during the Cambrian explosion mark the top. “It’s called the Great Unconformity, and it’s a gap in the geologic record,” says Nicolas Flament, a geoscientist at the University of Wollongong, Australia. “This gap can be anywhere between 200 million years, which is not that impressive, to more than a billion years.”

In a recent Nature study, Flament and his colleague Santosh Dhungana argue that this gap was carved with help from deep within the planet. According to their models, slow currents of hot rock in Earth’s mantle pushed entire continents up for hundreds of millions of years, letting glaciers grind them down and then let them sink, allowing fresh sediments to bury the scar. The largest hole in the rock record might be a fingerprint of processes happening deep in the planet’s interior.

The oldest sediments resting on the Great Unconformity in North America are between 635 and 482 million years old. Geologists have traced it across most of North America and parts of northern Europe and northern China. “Something seems to have happened, in particular in North America,” Flament says.

The leading explanation was glaciation. Between roughly 720 and 635 million years ago, glaciers reached the tropics, and some scientists think the entire planet froze over, a scenario called Snowball Earth. “Ice sheets are very good at grinding, scouring, scraping the surface,” Flament explains. He said that glaciers are about 10 times more effective than rivers at eroding rock away. But if the whole planet froze, the Great Unconformity should be found everywhere. And it’s not.

Other explanations included rifting, pulling and stretching of Earth’s crust, and a global drop in sea levels. But rifting acts mostly on the edges of continents, and a sea level change should have affected all continents alike. “There’s no doubt that there was extensive glaciation. There’s no doubt that there have been sea-level changes throughout time. The question is, was that enough to put the Great Unconformity puzzle together?” Flament says.

A fourth idea held that the mantle itself was moving continents up and down, a process called dynamic topography. “It had been proposed, but it hadn’t been quantitatively tested,” Flament says. So, his team set out to do so.

From roughly 100 to 200 kilometers beneath the surface down to nearly 3,000 kilometers, Earth’s mantle slowly convects. “An analogy would be a lava lamp, where some stuff comes up and some stuff comes down,” Flament explains. “Cold rock sinks and hot rock rises.”

The mantle is solid, yet a piece of rock can slowly travel from the surface to the deep Earth over some 200 million years. That motion tugs on the surface. Where cold slabs of ocean floor sink into the mantle at subduction zones, the ground gets pulled down; where hot mantle rises, it gets pushed up. The amplitude of this up and down motion is still debated, though. “The short of it is several hundreds of meters, and it’s still to be worked out exactly what that [precise value] is,” Flament says.

Dhungana and Flament ran six simulations of mantle convection going back 1 billion years, driven by reconstructions of how tectonic plates moved over that time. Then they grouped regions where the ground rose and fell in similar ways to find where the unconformity should have formed, and where it should have survived to the present. Flament, however, says the starting configuration of these simulations remains uncertain.

Magnetic records in ancient rocks reveal how far continents were from the equator, but not their longitude. “It’s possible that some continents may be in the wrong hemisphere,” Flament admits. The ancient ocean floor, long since recycled into the mantle, had to be invented entirely from scratch in the models. Still, the simulations produced a consistent story.

Laurentia, the ancient core of North America, had little or no subduction along its margins between about 930 and 510 million years ago, much like Africa today, so it sat above some rising mantle and was pushed up. Five of the six models showed this pattern of Laurentia seeing long uplift followed by sinking in the Cambrian. For northern Europe and North China, this pattern showed up in four.

But the uplift alone, Flament’s team concluded, was too gentle to strip away enough rock to create the Great Unconformity.

“The uplift could create a tilt for the whole continent, but it’s not great at creating a steep slope,” Flament says. The missing piece of the puzzle, he argues, were glaciers that ground through rock even on low ground and gentle slopes. The models show the uplift was still building when Snowball Earth hit. “This gentle uplift that’s continuous, and then that glacial erosion that comes on top, work together,” Flament explains. “They’re not coupled. They just happen to coincide in time.” When the continents sank in the Cambrian, seas flooded in and buried the eroded surface.

The models made by Flament’s team also explain why the gap is so obvious in North America but hard to find elsewhere. Compared with records of sediment preserved on each continent, North America and Eurasia show one long rise followed by one long fall, which the researchers interpret as the ideal recipe for the Great Unconformity.

South America and Australia, on the other hand, went through two cycles. “This would form potentially two unconformities, and then the Great Unconformity would be more difficult to see,” Flament says. Africa was a different case altogether—hemmed in by mountain belts at the time, its erosion and sediment were shaped by the mountains rather than by the mantle beneath.

But if the mantle goes through such up and down cycles on a regular basis, the remaining question was why we can see only one Great Unconformity, and not many.

The team says the Great Unconformity is unique in the geological record because of a combination of a rise-and-fall cycle lasting over 900 million years, a near-global glaciation that happened at the right moment, and rock records that get harder to read farther back in time.

Flament suspects there may be an older candidate, tied to a glaciation 2.4 billion years ago, but rocks that old lack the fossils that make younger sediments easier to date. “Having life in sediments makes it a lot easier to have a sense of time,” he says. His work, however, may help explain what happens right after the Great Unconformity formed.

The intense erosion that carved the Great Unconformity washed rock material into the oceans, possibly fertilizing them just before the Cambrian explosion, when calcium became abundant in seawater. “That’s when life learns to mobilize calcium and create skeletons and shells,” Flament says. He admits, though, that mantle dynamics likely wasn’t the only process that enabled the Cambrian explosion. “I think it would be misleading to say the mantle set the pace here. But I think it was one of the forces that worked together.”

Nature, 2026.  DOI: 10.1038/s41586-026-11156-5

Post-Trail Blues: Learning to Live Off the Trail

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

I miss the trail. It went by too fast. Where did the time go? Sometimes, I feel like I must have blacked out somewhere in the middle because suddenly, I was in New England. It’s hard to wrap my head around the fact that I did something so incredible, so challenging, and so far beyond what I ever thought I was capable of.

I would do anything to be back out there.

Smiling somewhere in a fire tower in New Jersey.

I never understood the people who thru-hike the Appalachian Trail multiple times. But the other morning, the thought crossed my mind, and for the first time, I understood the desire. No, I don’t think I would ever actually hike the entire trail again. But to quiet the mental battles I’m facing right now? To find that peace again? I would do it in a heartbeat.

I miss my friends. I miss who I was out there. I miss the simplicity of it all, the freedom of not constantly stressing about the future or wondering how I’m going to remain happy, fulfilled, and inspired. Out there, I didn’t have to have everything figured out. I just had to keep moving forward, one day and one mile at a time.

Coming back to the real world has been harder than I expected. People don’t look at me the same way, or maybe I’m just seeing things differently now. I don’t know if it’s envy, indifference, or if people are simply caught up in their own battles. Some people have no idea what I just accomplished, and others can’t seem to wrap their heads around it.

I’d be lying if I said I wasn’t disappointed. I wish more people asked me about my hike. I wish more people were curious about what I experienced, what I learned, and what it took to get through it. I wish more of the people who said they would join me had shown up along the way.

Words of encouragment outside of Hanover, NH

I think a part of me expected to come home and have people want to hear every little detail, to understand just how much this meant to me.

But I also know that life doesn’t revolve around me, my experiences, or my wants and needs. Everyone has their own lives to live and their own battles to fight. Just because this experience changed me doesn’t mean everyone else will know how to understand it.

I talked about these feelings with other thru-hikers who were experiencing something similar.  A few mentioned that this was one of the reasons they blogged. Writing gave them an outlet for sharing everything they’d experienced, a place to tell their stories and get all those thoughts out of their heads. Hopefully, those stories find an audience who cares, but even if they don’t, there’s something valuable about having a place to put it all.

That really resonated with me and inspired me to start writing for The Trek again. Maybe this is another way to stay connected to the trail, revisit those memories, and share a little piece of the experience with others.

And now, here I am, trying to figure out what comes next.

What am I going to do with my life? How do I hold on to the person I was out there? How do I find that same sense of purpose, peace, and inspiration when I’m back in a world that feels so much louder and more complicated?

Looking for moose on Ethan Pond in New Hampshire

And perhaps the question that keeps creeping into my mind: How soon can I hike another long-distance trail?

I knew coming home would be hard, but I don’t think I fully understood just how much I would miss the trail until I was no longer on it. I thought finishing would feel like an ending worth celebrating. Instead, it feels like I left a part of myself out there somewhere, and I’m still trying to figure out how to bring it home.

Born a Hoosier and now proudly calling Vermont home, I spend my off-trail days designing socks at Darn Tough Vermont. You’ll usually find me outside, chasing miles or identifying birds by sight, song, and attitude. I’ve completed the Indiana “Hoosier” Triple Crown, the Long Path, Northville–Placid Trail, and the Wonderland Trail. I'm also in the process of piecing together the Cohos Trail, Long Trail Side-to-Side, and NH48.

Stylist Soki Mak on 10 Things That Inspired Kim Kardashian’s <i><em>All’s Fair</em></i> Season Two Wardrobe

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

From 1990s Helmut Newton shoots to classic Bond girls to unexpected Comme des Garçons finds

In fashion, anything from niche archival research to a favorite film to an outfit you saw on your morning commute can make for inspiration. In our new series 10 Things, we ask industry professionals and tastemakers to divulge the (you guessed it) 10 things that had the most impact on their aesthetic choices.

The second season of Ryan Murphy’s legal drama, All’s Fair, opened with reality star-turned-actor Kim Kardashian in a patchwork Alexander McQueen suit. The rare ensemble, composed of a peplum jacket and fluted skirt, came from Lee McQueen’s theatrical Spring 2004 collection, titled Deliverance, in which models danced down the runway in shimmering gowns, dramatic feathered skirts, and tulle galore. “There were comments about Kim’s clothing being inappropriate in the workplace, but that’s what it should be,” Soki Mak, the visionary behind Kardashian’s Allura Grant character, told Harper’s Bazaar. “If I’m going to watch a show, I want to be like, ‘What the hell is that?’”

When Mak first joined the All’s Fair team—handpicked by Kardashian, via Instagram, to dress her onscreen as a high-powered lawyer—she had never ventured into costume design. The stylist, who has worked with everyone from Rosalía to Pamela Anderson, spent the first season developing the visual identity of Allura, merging her client’s own flair for shock value and fantasy with the limited character descriptions she received. We saw hyper-cinched waists and archival Jean Paul Gaultier; Hermès Birkins and suits with exposed thongs. It was Kardashian, with a twist.

But for Season Two, Mak focused on separating Kardashian from her character, tracking Allura’s emotional journey through darker color palettes and less expected silhouettes. She dug deeper into designer archives, emerging with moody gems from some of the most beloved collections of the ’90s and early-aughts. Rare vintage pieces from Maison Margiela, Comme des Garçons, Yohji Yamamoto, and Alaïa each made their cameos.

Ahead, the stylist dives into 10 influences that shaped Allura’s costume design, from Kim Kardashian herself to classic Bond girls to a baker-boy cap she had to fight for.

[For Season One,] no one gave us any sort of visual references. I’d never been a costume designer before, but usually, when I’ve worked on character-based stuff—I used to work a lot with Nadia Lee Cohen—we would develop the character first, so when I go into styling, it’s quite easy to visualize that person. There was no time for that, and there was no visual reference for Allura. So, when I came in, I was just thinking, “Okay, what do I see her as?” I looked at Kim personally, and she’s this fantasy. There is no one like her. So I’m like, “Okay, if you have all of the money in the world and all of the access in the world, all the power in the world, what would you look like?”

I’ve always thought of Kim as someone that creates and shocks and is the first to do something, whether people like it or not. And that’s the energy I wanted to go into this with. She challenges the norm. I’ve also always been a big believer in trying to be a trendsetter rather than a follower. In some of these moments, I would find it difficult to make a mood board because the references weren’t there. That was where I would say to Kim, “Trust me.”

For Season One, we started reaching out to brands, and Jean Paul Gaultier was [the first to say], “Let’s do it.” The minute we had them, the whole narrative of the show changed. They went deep into the archives and started sending these looks that hadn’t been worn in 20 years. It was amazing. We were sourcing vintage and archival pieces on the side, but we really focused on Jean Paul Gaultier because everything else compared to that just looked sad.

When the images started circulating from the first season, a critic that I respect wrote that the fashion was “kind of obvious.” Although I disagreed, I understood where he was coming from. Maybe Kim wearing a Mugler suit that accentuated her body and her curves was something we had seen her wear before. This season, I wanted to do something really different. I wanted to go less out-there with the colors and a little less predictable with the shapes and the styling. I picked designers and pieces that weren’t obvious choices, and I think it showed growth from last season. We took it somewhere else completely.

For hair and makeup references, I would go on Pinterest and pull old Helmut Newton shoots because I wanted it to look very timeless. I think that was the key. Everything looked … not minimal, there was definitely no minimal… but very ’90s-based and gorgeous.

Kim loves Madonna. There were a lot of old Madonna images for her platinum hair and makeup, a lot of which was done by Pat McGrath.

I think I’d made a point this year that I wanted to take it down a dark route. I almost wanted Allura to be like, “I just had a shit experience in the first season My man’s left me. I’m pissed off. I’ve gone away, and I’ve come back, and I’m serious now.” So everything’s much more covered up. There are darker layers. She’s experimenting. When I was going through the [recent] runway shows, everything was very pink and quite eclectic, so we mainly went with archival pieces. I used a lot of really rare Yohji Yamamoto, Comme des Garçons, and Lee McQueen.

There was a Margiela skirt suit [from the Spring 1994 collection, Retrospective] and it was just very kitsch. It was a long skirt with a suit jacket and was paired with a black leather baker boy hat from Hermès. We were in a bidding war with a collector in Japan. When we were filming the scene, someone in production or maybe Ryan [Murphy] had mentioned they wanted to tone down the hats for this season. But I got that memo after styling the look, and I was adamant that the look needed the hat. I begged. And then she sat at the table wearing a hat and I could see everyone was like, “How is she getting to wear a hat?” I just felt like the look needed it. It took [Kardashian] out of her normal visual stance and how she usually portrays herself.

Comme des Garçons is very destructive and not really tailored to fit a woman’s body in the way [Kardashian] usually likes. And we got her in [a gray jacket and long lilac-gray column skirt] and worked it around her body. She never once made any comments. She would just look at herself for a second and be like, “Cool.” And then go on set.

I have to let Kris Jenner take credit for [Elizabeth Taylor’s 1969 Scorpio cape, worn by Kardashian in Episode 1], because that’s her cape. She would come to set all the time and once had a conversation with Ryan about the fact that she has this cape. He was like, “That’s it, we need it.” From there, we shipped in a $10 million diamond that was most similar to what Elizabeth Taylor was wearing [in original imagery]. It was flown into L.A. in someone’s pocket, and we had it on set for like five days with Kim’s security. It’s a piece of history. It’s really special. Each day, it would go to Kim and then back in her car home because no one wanted to take responsibility for it.

In general, a lot of my choices in clothing and costume referenced old Bond films where the women were super sexual and curvy and had Brigitte Bardot hair. There was glamour, sex, opulence, money. The outfits were completely inappropriate and not really wearable in everyday life. But if I’m going to watch a show, that’s what I want to see. I want to be like, “What the hell? What is that?” There were comments about Kim’s clothing being inappropriate in the workplace, but that’s what it should be. It’s fun.

Justin Sun Warns Quantum Threat Could Hit Crypto Within 2 Years as TRON Pursues Resistance

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Quantum computing is advancing faster than many expect, TRON CEO Justin Sun says. While most people believe quantum computing is still far away, Justin Sun today said crypto attacks are looming.

In an interview organized by FINTECH.TV, Justin Sun said the dangers posed by quantum computers are real and could affect cryptocurrencies within the next year or two. The TRON CEO anticipates that breakthroughs in quantum computing infrastructure are making machines more powerful. This means computers’ ability to break cryptographic systems is becoming more imminent.

The executive recognized potential dangers to public-key cryptography, the backbone of blockchain networks. Crypto networks such as Bitcoin, Ethereum, and many others rely on cryptographic signatures like ECDSA, which are more vulnerable. According to Sun, advancing, powerful, fault-tolerant quantum computers could soon use Shor’s algorithm to break cryptographic signatures more easily than people think.

Currently, most crypto assets are kept in addresses with exposed public keys, putting them at risk of quantum computer attacks. Sun warns the imminent threat is likely within two years, as attackers can collect encrypted data now and decrypt it as quantum computing technology advances.

Today’s quantum computers can’t break cryptographic networks. However, the threat is growing as the technology becomes more powerful. This explains why Justin Sun said TRON is working on post-quantum cryptography.

Last month, the network announced that post-quantum cryptography is available on its testnet. The development marked a significant step toward preparing its infrastructure for potential advances in quantum computing.

This commitment matters because experts project that quantum computing could eventually overpower some of the cryptographic methods currently used to safeguard crypto assets. Post-quantum cryptography aims to build security controls created to resist attacks from powerful quantum computers.

8 Dreamy Hotels Our NYC Editors Are Booking This Winter

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

If there’s one thing the editors at Condé Nast Traveler love, it’s weekendmaxxing. We’re all about scouting the very best hotels upstate, or catching a quick flight to Miami, all before returning back to our New York City office by Monday. The art of the weekend trip becomes especially important in the wintertime, when the city closes its blinds and hibernates until spring. This is when an off-season jaunt to the Rockaways, or a hideaway in a Berkshires lodge, becomes all the more appealing. Essentially, we love a trip where you can get cozy somewhere else—because sometimes all you need is a simple change of scenery to tackle the winter blues.

From wellness sanctuaries nestled in the Catskills to country retreats in New England, these are the hotels our editors are eyeing for when the weather turns brisk, all within a few hours of NYC or a short flight away.

Where are you staying? Mayflower Inn & Spa, Washington, Connecticut

What drew you to this hotel? I visited Mayflower Inn back in spring 2024 with one of my best friends while she was pregnant with her second baby, and I’ve been dying to go back ever since. I don’t know if I’ve ever felt more relaxed than I did while there—we spent hours in the spa, chatted by the firepits, stuffed ourselves with farm-fresh meals, and feasted our eyes on some incredible interior design.

What are you eating? I won’t be leaving the hotel for food, that’s for sure. I’m always impressed by the F&B at Auberge properties, and the Mayflower Inn was no exception. The Garden Room offers creative but approachable seasonal cuisine from breakfast through dinner (don’t skip the burger), and the Tap Room is where you’ll find a tavern-style food menu plus a mixologist slinging delicious cocktails and mocktails.

What do you hope to get up to in the area? The Mayflower Inn is located in Washington, Connecticut, a small town in Litchfield County, a region beloved by many of my colleagues. Next time I visit the hotel, I’ll have to make a few stops along the way to see what all the fuss is about.

Where are you staying? Urban Cowboy Lodge, Big Indian, New York

What drew you to this hotel? Urban Cowboy Lodge has been on my weekend getaway wish list for years, and there’s something about it in winter that feels particularly charming—maybe it’s the delightful snow-filled view from the bathtub (pictured above) that drew me in. I’m imagining myself curled up with a good book by the lounge’s fireplace, draped in a Pendleton blanket, then dipping into the aforementioned copper clawfoot bathtubs. At just about two-and-a-half hours north of Manhattan, it’s a not-too-far excursion I’m saving for when I really need a break from winter in the city.

What are you eating? Phoenicia Diner, of course. I’m also keen to try out some of the many craft beers at Ralph’s Bar.

What do you hope to get up to in the area? I’m not a skier, so in the winter, heading to the Catskills comes with more of a stay-indoors-and-get-cozy vibe for me. Besides a pit stop at Phoenicia Diner, I imagine the furthest I’ll trek is over to the sauna and bowling alley.

Where are you staying? The Rockaway Hotel & Spa, Rockaway Park, New York

What drew you to this hotel? Aside from the ocean views—poetically perfect for New York in the winter—The Rockaway is home to one of my favorite wellness scenes in the city. The space transforms its outdoor pool area (which, in the summer months, is home to outdoor concerts and DJ-led pool parties) into the cozy Winter Pool House. The space gets decked out with outdoor cedar barrel saunas and the softest plush robes to wear while you transition to the heated outdoor pool. The warm water mixed with the ice-cold breeze coming off the nearby coastline is glorious.

What are you eating? I’ll be heading to Margie’s—the mood-lit hotel lobby restaurant—and ordering a juicy cheeseburger and a frosty gin martini. What better way to warm your soul?

What do you hope to get up to in the area? The beauty of visiting this hotel in winter? No summer beach crowds. I’ll likely bundle up, head to the beach for some meditation, and head right back to my cozy room at The Rockaway to soak up the views even more.

Where are you staying? Wildflower Farms, Auberge Collection, Gardiner, New York

What drew you to this hotel? I’ve had Wildflower Farms on my wish list for the past few years. Every photo I see of the property on Instagram looks so dreamy. It feels like a true escape from the city—quiet, cozy, and restful. Located in New York’s Hudson Valley, it’s a quick 90-minute drive from NYC, making it a convenient pick for couples or families seeking an easy getaway.

What are you eating? I’m most eager to have coffee and pastries around the fire at the Great Porch, Wildflower’s open-air communal space with sprawling views of the Shawangunk Ridge. And of course, I’ll be dining at the resort’s on-site restaurant, Clay, where the rustic American menu is full of seasonal ingredients from local farms.

What do you hope to get up to in the area? I usually try to pack as many activities and must-try coffee shops and restaurants as possible into my trip itinerary, but Wildflower Farms encourages you to slow down. You’ll find me at Thistle, the resort’s on-site spa where hand-harvested, small-batch oils and scrubs are used during tailored treatments.

Where are you staying? Inness, Accord, New York

What drew you to this hotel? Inness is resplendent in the summer, so I’ve always been keen to see a different side of it come hunker-down weather. Its cabins are certainly primed for rest, thanks to the fireplaces, but there’s also the outdoor pit with prime-time views of the Catskill Mountains decked in snow. I also love the central farmhouse, where cozy seats call out for a great book and a hot cocoa. And if you can drag yourself out, which you should, there are loads of hikes and walks, including those along the Ashokan Reservoir, which is one of my favorite spots.

What are you eating? Stacks of pancakes; comfort-food entrées like risottos; a round of Dorato Negronis at the bar.

What do you hope to get up to in the area? Spending the afternoons shopping and dining in some of my favorite nearby towns, like Kingston and Woodstock.

Where are you staying? Four Seasons Hotel at The Surf Club, Surfside, Florida

What drew you to this hotel? When one of my best friends announced she was celebrating her 30th birthday in Miami, a city I’ve shockingly never been to, the first place I thought of was the Four Seasons Hotel at the Surf Club; I remember reading about its incredible celebrity guest history, which includes everyone from Frank Sinatra to Elizabeth Taylor.

What are you eating? Perhaps we’d start with a toast at the on-site Champagne Bar, then move on over to dinner at Lido for seafood-inspired pastas.

What do you hope to get up to in the area? Laze around by the pool, enjoy the quieter beaches of Surfside, and maybe stroll through the Rubell Museum in Allapattah.

Where are you staying? Tourists, North Adams, Massachusetts

What drew you to this hotel? I love heading north of the city in the colder months—leaning in and getting even colder and cozier. And the words “reimagined motor lodge” are another form of catnip for me. I love the sense of nostalgia paired with very plush, updated interiors, ideal for hiding out for a wintery weekend.

What are you eating? I’d carve out time for the hotel’s restaurant, the Airport Room, ideally timed to one of the chef pop-ups they do—though given the three-and-a-half-hour drive from New York City, I’d probably also plot out an en route lunch in Kingston as well.

Salmon Is 10x More Delicious When You Add A Spoonful Of This Condiment

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Salmon tends to be a heavier, rich fish that benefits from bright ingredients that lighten its final flavor profile. That's why a simple Dijon-crusted salmon has so much going for it: It combines the best of hearty seafood and sharp tang with a breadcrumb sprinkle that guarantees deliciousness.

Mustard is the flavorful binder that makes breaded meat less boring, but it works wonders on any type of protein — whether you're using it for your fish or to slow-cook a pork shoulder. But Dijon goes so far beyond the standard yellow condiment you may use for burgers and hot dogs. Made with dark seeds, vinegar, and splashes of white wine, it's a complex blend of sinus-opening heat, slow fermentation, and earthy herbs. Not only does it cut through the fat of even the most high-quality salmon, but all these tastes together also make it a fairly comprehensive flavoring agent, removing the need for complex seasoning blends and cooking styles.

Whether you're working with farmed or wild salmon, Dijon is the best choice. Wild-caught fish have more complexity, thanks to their oceanic diets, and just a light smear of Dijon provides an equally nuanced ingredient to play off its natural tastes. Farm-raised varieties are often fattier, so a heavier application ensures your filets don't end up tasting too rich. Regardless of the type, mustard provides a potent adhesive for any seasonings you do choose to use, giving your delicate protein a delicious crust and caramelizing into a crispy brown topping.

Just because Dijon is plenty tasty in its own right doesn't mean you can't tweak it for specific recipes. Whether you're pairing it with your breadcrumbs or looking to improve your meat directly, treat it like a compound butter and mix in whatever you have lying around for an easy upgrade.

Both salmon and Dijon are perfect candidates to pair with herbs. However, since fish typically doesn't need long cooking times, it's best to stick with fresh, rather than dried, which take longer for the flavor to bloom. Minced dill, basil, or thyme are easy to add to Dijon and complement its winey notes and pungency. Be careful not to use too much, or anything too potent, as it can work with the Dijon to overpower your fish and make it far more difficult to taste. It may help to whisk a bit of oil into the mustard first, so the herbs and spices bloom in the oven, even under the additional weight of breadcrumbs.

Though some consider it a bit taboo, creating a mustard-cheese blended condiment is a truly next-level way to upgrade your filets — plus, in many cases, seafood and cheese belong together. Mild, high-fat, soft varieties like cream cheese, mascarpone, and camembert blend well with Dijon and won't make a too powerful condiment, though a sprinkle of parmesan or pecorino on top can add some valuable earthiness. This creates great flavor, especially if you abstain from breadcrumbs. The more direct heat helps the mustard-cheese combo caramelize into a deliciously decadent shell, and the excess fat pools right off onto the pan.

2028 Subaru Getaway

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

See all results for new 2028 Subaru Getaway for sale near 20147

Subaru's latest EV is the 2028 Getaway, the brand's largest electric SUV yet. With three rows of seats and over 300 miles of claimed driving range, the Getaway seems destined for weekend trips with the family, so the name tracks. If your road-trip adventure involves towing, the Getaway can tug up to 3500 pounds of trailer, but as with all EVs, your driving range will suffer significantly when hauling a load.

A standard dual-motor all-wheel drive powertrain provides 420 horsepower, and the Getaway can recharge using the Tesla Supercharger network thanks to a standard NACS charging port. Passenger space in all three rows is on par with segment rivals such as the Kia EV9 and the Hyundai Ioniq 9, and cargo capacity is generous too.

Expert Tip: If you'd prefer a Toyota EV SUV, look at the Highlander EV. It's mechanically and stylistically similar to the Getaway, as it's part of a joint venture between the two automakers.

The Getaway was initially slated to reach customers by the end of 2026 as a 2027 model, but delays have pushed those plans back. We now expect to see the Getaway at dealerships in early 2027 as a 2028 model.

If this year's model is largely unchanged and you're comfortable with a lightly used car, you may want to consider shopping previous model years. A one-to-three-year-old model will likely have a warranty and it'll cost less than buying new.

All Getaway models are all-wheel drive and powered by a pair of electric motors that combine for 420 horsepower. Performance should be more than suitable for its family-hauling mission. While its twin, the Toyota Highlander EV, is offered with a single-motor front-wheel drive variant, the Subaru doesn't offer that setup. When we get a chance, we'll take the Getaway to our test track and update this story with test results and driving impressions.

In Comparison: Subaru's claimed 60 mph time is in the same ballpark as other three-row electric SUVs we've tested. An all-wheel-drive EV9 did it in 4.5 seconds, and an all-wheel-drive Ioniq 9 was slightly quicker at 4.4 seconds.

In Comparison: The Getaway's maximum towing capacity falls short of the Kia EV9's 5000-pound max.

Two different battery packs are available, starting with a 77-kWh pack as the standard range battery. Driving range hasn't been estimated for that model yet, but with the larger 96-kWh pack, Subaru says the Getaway should be able to go for over 300 miles between charges. There's a NACS charging port standard, so the Getaway can charge at Tesla Superchargers. On a 150-kW connection, Subaru estimates charging time to be 30 minutes from 10 to 80 percent.

First Row: The Getaway's interior is comfortable and relatively stylish. A host of desirable features are standard, including heated front seats with power adjustments, three-zone automatic climate control, and customizable ambient interior lighting. Fake leather upholstery is standard on the Premium and Limited trims, but the Touring model comes with soft, nappa leather on the seats.

Second Row: A pair of captain's chairs is standard on the base Premium trim and the loaded Touring, but optional on the mid-range Limited. The Limited gets a three-across bench as standard, but we expect most people will want the bucket seats.

Third Row: There's a two-seat third row in every Getaway model. While it will likely only be used by kids and teens, we climbed back there to see if an adult could ride comfortably. Space is a little tight, but it can accommodate adults for short trips. Third row space is on par with rivals such as the Kia EV9 and Hyundai Ioniq 9. When not needed, the third-row folds flat into the floor and is power-operated via buttons located in the cargo area.

Cargo Capacity: Subaru says there's 16 cubic feet of storage space behind the third row and 46 cubic feet with the third row folded. When we get a chance, we'll measure the Getaway's cargo capacity by stuffing it full of our carry-on suitcases.

A 14.0-inch touchscreen is standard and runs the same infotainment interface that we've tried in other Subaru electric vehicles. It's generally intuitive and easy to operate. Bluetooth, Apple CarPlay, Android Auto, and a 12.3-inch gauge display are all standard.

A pair of smartphone charging pads is located on the center console, and there are four USB-C charging ports located in the second and third rows. A six-speaker stereo is standard, but the top-spec Touring trim comes with a 13-speaker Harman/Kardon stereo instead.

The Getaway comes with Subaru's EyeSight driver assistance tech, which includes lane-keeping assist, automated emergency braking, blind-spot monitoring, and adaptive cruise control.

For information about the Getaway's crash-test results, visit the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) and Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS) websites.

Subaru offers average warranty coverage. However, the brand doesn't offer any complimentary scheduled maintenance.

The 2027 Karma Gyesera Is a Stylish Curiosity

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Gyesera replaces the Revero, itself a descendant of the original Fisker Karma, with a sultry design and an updated plug-in hybrid powertrain.

Henrik Fisker probably had no idea how fitting the name Karma would be when he launched the plug-in hybrid sedan in 2011 under his eponymous startup. In Hinduism, karma is intrinsically linked to reincarnation, the karma accumulated in one life affecting the conditions of the next life. Fisker’s venture wound up failing in 2014, but after Wanxiang Group, a Chinese automotive parts conglomerate, bought the assets, the car was reborn as the Karma Revero. The Revero has been built since 2016 and has undergone several updates, but the tiny California-based company has struggled to gain a foothold.

Now Karma Automotive is seeking to reinvent itself, and it will start by giving the plug-in hybrid sedan yet another reincarnation. Marques McCammon—previously of DaimlerChrysler, Roush, Saleen, and engineering firm Ricardo—joined as president of the company in 2023, and the 2027 Gyesera is the first product launched under his guidance. While it retains similarities to the sedans that preceded it, a lightly updated powertrain and a fresh design language aim to set the brand up for a brighter future. Driving a pre-production version of the Gyesera in and around Las Vegas, we discovered a car that feels special and provides enticing powertrain flexibility but that is still bound by some of the same flaws as its predecessors.

See all results for used cars for sale near 29405

The Gyesera was initially revealed in 2024 as a fully electric vehicle, but Karma Automotive pivoted when it determined that the luxury market wasn’t moving toward EVs fast enough. Instead of the originally proposed 590-hp battery-electric setup, the Gyesera now launches with a fifth generation of the Karma’s plug-in hybrid powertrain. A turbocharged 1.5-liter three-cylinder engine—borrowed from the BMW i8—lives under the hood, but it isn’t connected to the wheels. Instead, it acts as a generator to charge the 28-kWh battery, which then feeds electricity to a pair of motors on the rear axle.

The Gyesera produces 566 horsepower—30 hp more than the Revero—and 594 pound-feet of torque, an increase of 44. Karma says the Gyesera accelerates from zero to 60 mph in 3.7 seconds, down from 4.5 seconds in the Revero, and can hit a 150-mph top speed, up from the Revero’s 125-mph V-max. The nickel-manganese-cobalt battery is also capable of providing a claimed 80 miles of electric-only driving, with a total range on gasoline of 360 miles. The battery can support DC fast-charging via a CCS port at up to 50 kilowatts or AC charging at 6.6 kilowatts.

As you approach the Gyesera, it makes a strong first impression. There remains more than a whiff of the original Fisker Karma in its appearance, with the Gyesera using an evolution of the aluminum spaceframe chassis found in previous Karmas. Design-wise, this is no bad thing. The proportions look exotic, with a hood that seems to go on forever and a roofline that tapers over the muscular rear haunches. The curvaceous body is fashioned from a mix of aluminum and carbon fiber, the latter comprising the hood, rear decklid, fenders, rocker panels, and optionally, the roof.

See all results for used cars for sale near 29405

While the shape is familiar, the design has been sharpened. Up front, a crisp line runs underneath piercing headlights, replacing the black grille trim that made the Revero look like an athlete wearing a mouthguard. The lower air intake is framed by a carbon-fiber trim piece, while a swooping indent, which Karma calls the Comet Line, arcs across the hood, giving the car a powerful look and serving as a hot air outlet for the engine bay. A strip of exposed, unpainted carbon fiber at the top of the hood is a reminder of the Gyesera’s high-end construction.

The forged aluminum wheels are the fruit of a partnership with HRE, with two different 21-inch designs available along with a 22-inch option. The smooth sides are broken up by a carbon-fiber trim piece carved into the front fender, and at the rear, slim taillights sit above a subtle diffuser. The Gyesera’s visual drama is even more apparent in person, and even amid the fancy cars in Vegas, the Gyesera turned heads on the Strip.

Inside, Karma revised the dashboard, ditching the Revero’s four circular air vents for rectangular vents hidden within a black trim piece that spans the dash. The 10.1-inch screen has been repositioned and is paired with a 12.3-inch digital gauge cluster. Some things remain unchanged from the Revero, such as the pyramid-like push-button gear selector and the steering wheel, which still has an echo of the original Fisker Karma’s design.

The highlight, though, is the cabin’s luxurious materials. The seats and dashboard feature sumptuous Bridge of Weir leather, while the center console, door armrests, and parts of the steering wheel are covered in soft Alcantara microsuede. The metallic trim feels solid and cool, but the glossy black plastic covering the center console is a low point.

Most functions are handled digitally, but we didn’t find it as infuriating as other screen-focused setups, in part because the screen is within easy reach of the driver. We still believe real buttons and dials feel more upscale, but the screen, which also supports wireless phone mirroring, wasn’t too fussy. To keep your phone topped up, there’s a wireless charger and USB-C ports hidden inside the armrest.

The battery is housed in what would traditionally be a transmission tunnel, allowing for a low seating position. The seats, however, are quite narrow, even for someone with an average build, and the unadjustable headrest is a bit too low. While we appreciated their copious bolstering in the corners, a wider, comfier seat would be more befitting a grand tourer.

The center console extends into the rear of the cabin, where there are two more bucket seats. While Karma claims the Gyesera has more legroom than the Revero, it hasn’t helped much. The rear seat is almost unusable for adults, with this 5-foot-11-inch writer barely able to squeeze in behind his own seating position, with his knees scrunched up and his head brushing the roof. The trunk is also quite tight, with a high floor and a fairly narrow opening.

The Gyesera is satisfying to drive for the most part. Every control has pleasing heft to it. The steering is decently communicative, and its weight builds naturally as you turn the wheel. The accelerator isn’t touchy, with the swell of power growing progressively as you press the pedal. The Gyesera weighs just over 5000 pounds and has a lengthy wheelbase, but it shrinks in the corners and feels nimble.

Although the Gyesera may not be able to match the heady power figures of other high-end electric-driven cars, it still packs plenty of verve. Launch it off the line, and the rear tires chirp before the torque-vectoring system finds traction and shoots you forward. Get too excited with the throttle coming out of a corner, and the rear end tries to break loose. It’s not terribly hard to overwhelm the Goodyear Eagle F1 tires, which measure 255/35R-22 up front and 285/35R-22 at the rear, giving the Gyesera a bit of a playful edge.

Braking is handled by Brembo 14-inch rotors, with six-piston calipers up front and four-piston calipers at the rear. The brakes are strong, although the car occasionally felt a bit skittish under heavy deceleration, and while the blending of regenerative braking and the friction brakes is decent, the pedal doesn’t give much feedback. The three levels of regenerative braking are adjusted by the right-side steering-wheel-mounted paddle. The weakest setting is very light, while the strongest setting is close to one-pedal driving but won’t bring the car to a complete stop.

The left paddle selects the drive mode: Stealth, Sustain, or Sport. In Stealth, the engine stays off and the Gyesera glides along on electric power. Sustain fires up the engine, running in its most efficient mode to maintain the battery charge level. The engine hums away in the background most of the time but occasionally gets intrusively loud.

In Sport mode, the engine works as hard as possible to replenish the battery. Sport also opens up the exhaust, but this is actually one of the Gyesera’s biggest flaws. The three-cylinder sounds, frankly, terrible, with a buzzy note and a flatulent thwack when you let off the throttle at high rpm. The engine is also constantly kicking on and off, making its rasp even more noticeable, since every time you lift, the cacophony completely falls away. The exhaust also exits right underneath the driver’s-side door, exacerbating the din. It’s a sound that’s more befitting a hot hatch than a luxury GT. The recently revealed one-off Karma Eximius 1 trades the three-cylinder for a 6.2-liter V-8, and we think a roaring V-8 would be far more fitting in the Gyesera.

The ride quality, however, helps restore the Gyesera’s upscale positioning. The car rides on Öhlins coil-over dampers and features unequal-length control arms at the front and rear. Despite not having adaptive dampers or air springs, the ride is smooth and comfortable, without feeling floaty, and the Gyesera still keeps body roll in check. It’s the ideal blend of comfort and agility, a ride that won’t punish you on rough surfaces but still provides plenty of confidence in the corners.

The Gyesera is, ultimately, a niche product, although that’s at least partially intentional. The first 50 units are set to reach dealerships before the end of this year, and the Gyesera will remain a low-production, handcrafted model. Prices start at $139,995, although the one we drove was optioned up to $158,995. That’s not cheap, and the powertrain isn’t nearly as rousing as those in high-performance sedans from Mercedes-AMG, Porsche, and BMW. But the Gyesera counters with a head-turning design, high-end materials, a well-balanced ride, and sporty handling, while also offering the feeling of instant electric torque and the ability to travel up to 80 miles without using gas. This incarnation of the slinky plug-in hybrid sedan is much improved and shows promise for the latest rebirth of Karma Automotive.

The 2027 Karma Gyesera is the latest evolution of the plug-in hybrid sedan that first arrived as the Fisker Karma way back in 2011, and it gets a revised powertrain and design as Karma Automotive aims to kick off a new era.

The Gyesera has the same dramatic proportions as its forebears, with a long hood and a curvaceous body, built around an evolution of the aluminum spaceframe chassis found in the Revero that it replaces.

The body is fashioned from a mix of aluminum and carbon fiber, helping keep the curb weight at just over 5000 pounds despite the Gyesera’s lengthy silhouette.

The Gyesera employs the fifth generation of Karma’s plug-in hybrid system, which uses a BMW turbocharged three-cylinder engine as a generator to maintain the charge of a 28-kWh battery.

That battery is hooked up to a pair of electric motors mounted on the rear axle, and the Gyesera produces 566 horsepower and 594 pound-feet of torque.

That’s 30 more horses and 44 more pound-feet than its predecessor, the Revero, allowing the Gyesera to hit 60 mph in a claimed 3.7 seconds and a top speed of 150 mph.

Karma says the battery provides an electric-only driving range of 80 miles; once the gas engine kicks off, range extends to 360 miles.

Teenage girl held captive for 500 days rescued after secretly asking for help in Among Us

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A teenage girl who was allegedly held captive for more than 500 days has been rescued after using Among Us to alert another player that she had been kidnapped.

Kylie Arellano, 17, disappeared from her home in Utah in May 2025 when she was just 15 years old. She was finally rescued on November 1, 2026, after contacting another child through the popular multiplayer game.

According to Newsweek, Arellano had been held against her will in the basement of a home belonging to 39-year-old Adam Hunter Harmel in Huntington, Oregon.

Federal prosecutors allege Harmel met the teenager online before driving to Utah to pick her up, throwing her phone into a river and taking her across state lines.

Once at his home, Harmel allegedly prevented Arellano from leaving, repeatedly assaulted her and forced her to remove her braces with pliers to make her harder to identify.

Despite allegedly keeping her captive, Harmel eventually provided Arellano with a phone that had no cellular service but could still connect to Wi-Fi.

In mid-October, she began communicating with another child through Among Us. Their conversations eventually moved to another social media platform, where Arellano revealed her identity and explained that she was being held against her will.

She also reportedly believed police had stopped searching for her.

The other player’s mother contacted the FBI, which arranged to monitor a video call with Arellano on November 1.

During the call, the teenager described her captivity and showed investigators evidence supporting her claims, including a knife Harmel allegedly kept beneath his pillow.

World of Warcraft account helps police locate missing 16-year-old

Among Us hackers targeted as InnerSloth rush out emergency hotfix

Authorities rescued Arellano from the Oregon property that same day and took her for medical treatment. Harmel was arrested during a traffic stop.

In a November 4 statement, the Department of Justice confirmed Harmel had been charged with transporting a minor across state lines with the intent to engage in criminal sexual activity.

The case comes amid growing concerns about predators targeting children through online gaming platforms.

Earlier in 2026, two sisters aged 12 and 15 were rescued after allegedly being kidnapped by a man they met on Roblox.

However, in Arellano’s case, an online gaming connection ultimately helped bring her captivity to an end.

Harmel made his initial federal court appearance on November 4.

No Alps, No Pyrenees, and No Paris. The 2027 Tour de France Femmes Will Still Deliver Exciting Racing, but the Women Deserve More of Cycling’s Iconic Backdrops.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A three-day start in England and a finish atop the Puy de Dôme give us plenty to look forward to. But some of the organizers’ choices leave me wondering what this Tour could have been.

The route of the 2027 Tour de France Femmes avec Zwift was revealed in late October, featuring a Grand Départ in the UK, a jaunt through central France, and a summit finish atop the Puy de Dôme. The sixth edition of the revived women’s Tour will kick off on Friday, July 30, and conclude on Saturday, August 7, after nine days of racing.

In just five editions, the Tour de France Femmes avec Zwift has become the pinnacle of the women’s WorldTour calendar. And looking at the 2027 route, we’re in for another nine days of aggressive racing, a hard-fought GC battle, and opportunities for a wide range of stage winners. Yet some route choices leave me lukewarm, and a few things are missing altogether. There’s no trek into the Alps or Pyrenees, no individual time trial, and still no Paris finale.

Now, I don’t doubt that the women’s peloton will make the most of the route it’s given, but whether it’ll match the spectacle of previous editions is another question.

First, the highlights, starting with the three-day Grand Départ in England.

The 2027 route puts a lot of the fireworks up front, so there’s no easing into this Tour. It opens with a short, punchy 86km stage from Leeds to Manchester. Officially classified as flat, it still packs in three categorized climbs. So while the sprinters may get their chance at the first yellow jersey, it’s not guaranteed. Then comes a bumpy stage two that, a local tells me, is much harder on the legs than it looks on paper. And then we’ll get the Tour de France Femmes avec Zwift’s first team time trial in London.

Leeds, Manchester, London. The 2027 edition will put women’s racing in front of millions. That’s a huge opportunity to draw roadside crowds and reach new audiences. Those opening stages could also give the GC an intriguing shape. An early winner backed by a strong team performance in London might carry a rider in yellow deep into the race.

Then there’s the Puy de Dôme. The volcano occupies a special place in cycling history, having hosted 14 men’s Tour de France stage finishes, including the famous 1964 head-to-head between Jacques Anquetil and Raymond Poulidor.

A more recent memory of the Puy de Dôme is that of Matteo Jorgenson’s heartbreak on stage nine of the 2023 Tour. The American attacked alone with almost 50km remaining, only for Canadian Michael Woods to catch and pass him some 450 meters from victory. Jorgenson ultimately finished fourth.

It’s easy to imagine the volcano hosting another exciting chapter in the Kasia Niewiadoma (Canyon//SRAM) and Demi Vollering (FDJ United–Suez) rivalry.

In 2025, the Tour de France Femmes avec Zwift delivered six different stage winners and three riders in yellow. This year, it was six and five respectively. The 2027 route should again give a broad cast of riders their chance, with plenty of terrain for versatile stage hunters alongside opportunities for the sprinters and climbers.

That’s something I like about the Tour. A Tour stage can define a rider’s season; just look at the breakthrough victories of Maëva Squiban (UAE Team L'Imad), Cédrine Kerbaol (EF Education–Oatly), and Kim Le Court-Pienaar (AG Insurance–Soudal) in years past.

The route opens the door to well-rounded riders, but the overall will still likely be decided on the final two mountainous days. But if climbing decides the Tour, I’d like to see some high-mountain racing in the Alps or Pyrenees. If the intention is to give more riders a shot at the GC, then I want to believe that they can actually take the yellow home, rather than just borrow it for a few days.

Of course, Vollering and Niewiadoma excel on punchy terrain too. And that’s part of my concern: different profiles don’t guarantee different outcomes. Perhaps the shorter climbs will keep more riders in contention, but I’m not convinced this route broadens the fight for yellow as much as it prolongs it.

Then there’s London. I’m excited about the setting, but the racing format leaves me underwhelmed. I prefer an individual time trial where every rider controls her own performance. No matter the timing rules, a team time trial tends to favor stronger, bigger teams, with more money to spend on a deeper roster, equipment, testing, and optimizing. A rider from a team with fewer resources could leave London with a time loss that reflects her team's budget more than her own performance.

Eighteen kilometers may limit the damage, but as 2024 showed, when Niewiadoma-Phinney won the overall by just four seconds, every single second counts.

While Britain gets the big-city showcases, I feel roadside fans in France get a bad deal. The route bypasses the country’s largest cities on its way to a finale where spectator access remains uncertain.

Strict environmental protections are in place on Puy de Dôme, and organizers are still exploring if they can admit a limited crowd at the summit. I understand the need for protection, but a repeat of 2023’s empty upper slopes would be a lonely way to welcome in the Tour winner. If fans aren’t welcome, surely it’s worth asking whether this is the right place to finish.

And while a summit finish can deliver exciting racing, I’m still waiting to see the women’s peloton finish on the cobblestones of Paris, same as the men. Wout van Aert’s victory in 2025 and Mathieu van der Poel’s all-out effort in 2026 were absolute highlights of those Tours for me. The Montmartre circuit has shown how much racing in Paris can offer, beyond a ceremonial procession. After watching Vollering and Niewiadoma duel in France and again in Montréal, with Marlen Reusser never far behind, I want to see what these women could do on those streets.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

crafted through advanced 3D printing using sustainable biopolymers, this series of table lamps, vases, and candle holders bridges digital precision with a touch of fairytale storytelling.

Gliese Design edited by christina vergopoulou November 6, 2026 2 min

Multidisciplinary designer Mustafa Başaran and his studio Gliese Design introduce the Ondu Wonderland Collection. Sourced from the studio’s broader material research into natural balance and geometric proportions, the collection comprises 3D printed table lamps, vases, and candle holders manufactured using sustainable biopolymers. Through additive manufacturing methods and additive layering, the series pairs computational precision with organic profiles, translating narrative themes into functional domestic objects designed to introduce balanced, low-impact materiality into contemporary living spaces.

the Ondu Wonderland table lamp pairs a ruffled silhouette with a chess-inspired base

The Ondu Wonderland Collection draws directly from the playful surrealism of Lewis Carroll, translating the ‘logical nonsense’ of Alice’s world into contemporary objects. Each piece features signature ruffled forms that echo the theatrical collars of the Victorian era, paired with precise checkerboard bases inspired by the timeless geometry of a chessboard.

Comprising a table lamp, a vase, and a candle holder, the collection merges the cold precision of digital 3D printing with the warmth of narrative design. Printed using eco-friendly PLA derived from corn starch and recycled food packaging, alongside post-consumer PETG, the series proves that high-tech manufacturing and environmental mindfulness can coexist seamlessly. More than just functional items, the Ondu Wonderland Collection by Gliese Design Studio acts as a storyteller in the interior space—inviting quiet curiosity and revealing new details with every glance.

US 401(k) proposal leaves door open to altcoins, but hurdles remain

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

While the proposal doesn’t exclude altcoins and smaller cryptocurrencies, experts say they could face challenges getting into retirement plans.

Smaller cryptocurrencies could become available in US 401(k) retirement plans under a proposed Labor Department rule, but experts say they may face more challenges than Bitcoin (BTC) and Ether (ETH).

The rule reached the White House for final review on Thursday, according to the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs. It aims to make it easier for retirement plans to offer alternative investments like crypto, while offering more legal protection for employers and plan managers.

The Labor Department’s March proposal neither specifies which digital assets could be included nor sets a minimum market capitalization requirement. However, it does guide retirement plan managers to consider six factors when choosing: performance, fees, liquidity, valuation, benchmarks and complexity.

The proposal could open the door to more cryptocurrencies in retirement plans, however, it does not guarantee they will be offered. And when it comes to smaller tokens, experts say they could face challenges because of their limited history, liquidity and other risks.

Teresa Goody Guillén, managing partner at Bellementis PLLC, said Bitcoin and Ether are likely to be the first cryptocurrencies offered because of their established markets and investment products. Bitcoin and Ether are the two largest cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, at approximately $1.5 trillion and $273 billion, respectively, according to CoinGecko data as of 20:01 UTC on Nov. 6.

Still, that doesn’t automatically exclude altcoins, she said. Instead, those managing 401(k) plans would need to show that their investment choices are sensible.

“The real question isn’t which cryptocurrencies regulators pick – it’s whether an investment meets the same standards of prudence, liquidity, valuation and investor protection as any other retirement option,” Guillén said.

She added that while a digital asset’s market cap matters, it shouldn’t be the deciding factor. “Big doesn’t mean suitable, and small doesn’t mean disqualified,” Guillén said. “Digital assets shouldn’t get special treatment, or be excluded because they use different technology.”

Bernardo Brites, co-founder and CEO of Trace Finance, echoed Guillén sentiment, adding: “Run any token through that list and the answer is short. Bitcoin passes clearly. Ether passes; everything else fails on track record or benchmark before a fiduciary ever gets to market cap.”

Brites explained, in this situation, market cap matters only indirectly. “The practical test is simpler: can a plan committee document a daily, reliable price from a regulated wrapper?” he said. “If not, it is not going in.”

While the Labor Department’s proposal does not limit crypto investments to exchange-traded funds (ETFs) or managed funds, experts said those products will likely be offered before direct crypto holdings.

“ETFs and managed funds will likely come first because they fit existing custody and recordkeeping systems,” Guillén said. Whereas holding cryptocurrencies directly would raise additional concerns about storage, cybersecurity and pricing, Guillén said. Still, she added that those risks should not automatically rule them out.

Brites also expects crypto investments to become available gradually. He said existing brokerage options would likely come first, followed by standalone ETFs and funds that include small amounts of crypto.

He expects some crypto investment options to become available in 2027, followed by ETFs at larger employers later that year. Crypto could become more widely available in retirement funds by 2028, he said.

“The rule changes the legal risk, not the plumbing, and the plumbing is slower,” Brites said. “White House review can take up to ninety days, then publication, then an effective date typically sixty days later.”

Brites further explained that legal uncertainty could also slow adoption. The proposal would offer retirement plan managers some protection from lawsuits, but it would not prevent them from being sued over their investment decisions.

He also warned that future administrations could reverse the rule, a possibility that could make employers hesitant to add crypto to long-term retirement plans.

“The 2022 guidance was reversed in 2025; a fiduciary signing a ten-year plan design has to consider that this rule could be reversed too,” he said. “And with courts giving less deference to agencies, how much weight the safe harbor carries in litigation is itself unsettled.”

California Passes Down Payment Assistance Plan, but Push To Cut Red Tape Remains in Limbo

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

California voters headed to the polls on Tuesday to decide three new housing-related ballot measures. While two of them handily passed, votes are still being counted on a plan to cut red tape and streamline environmental reviews for new homes.

Voters approved Proposition 1, which authorizes California to issue $11.25 billion in bonds to support housing affordability programs, including multifamily rental housing, permanent supportive housing, and affordable housing. The funding also backs both down payment assistance programs and mortgage programs for veterans.

Gov. Gavin Newsom expressed support for the measure, as did the Democratic Party of California. The ACLU, the California Apartment Association, and the California Housing Consortium also supported the measure, although there was some scattered opposition from Republican state legislators.

"This bond is about building communities, expanding access and affordability in California, where every family has a fair shot at a place to call home," Newsom said in a June statement.

Voters backed a similar measure in 2024 that earmarked $6.38 billion for homelessness and veterans housing programs.

"Higher mortgage rates have squeezed homebuyers and the for-sale market for four years, and they have hurt builders, too, especially in multifamily," Realtor.com senior economist Joel Berner says.

"Apartment projects depend on layers of financing, and assembling it has gotten much harder at today’s borrowing costs, particularly in a state as expensive and complicated to build in as California. When rates jumped in 2022 and 2023, a backlog of [COVID-19] pandemic-era projects that were already financed kept delivering homes. That backlog has been worked through, and the multifamily pipeline behind it is much thinner today," Berner adds.

Berner calls Prop 1 a "modest attempt to plug this gap" but only "one piece of the puzzle," noting that the infusion of cash won't do much to reduce approval times or land acquisition costs.

Californians also approved Proposition 37, which creates a $25 billion loan program for middle-income earners to purchase new homes. The state would issue bonds and use the proceeds to offer fixed-rate mortgages for up to 17% of the purchase price of newly constructed homes priced below $1.5 million

Borrowers must be California residents who pay at least 3% down and meet some income requirements. This, supporters say, would help lower the amount they'd need to save for a down payment on a new home. They expect the measure to spur the construction of new homes and be paid for when investors and home borrowers repay the loans.

The California Association of Realtors and the California Chamber of Commerce both supported the measure.

"Voters in California have seized the opportunity to help alleviate the burden of current mortgage rates on new-home buyers," says Berner of the initiative's passage. "With current mortgage rates holding above 7%, this presents the opportunity for buyers to save on interest payments for part of their home purchase, which could be a boon to the new-home segment of sales in the Golden State."

But, says Berner, housing is still above $1.5 million in many California metros, signaling that the measure's impact may be circumscribed.

"The median listing price for newly built single-family homes in the San Jose metro is over $2.1 million. In Los Angeles, it’s over $1.7 million. San Francisco has a median of about $1.5 million, and San Diego’s median is about $1.3 million. Among newly built single-family homes for sale in 2026 in the San Jose metro, just 41.7% were priced below $1.5 million. In Los Angeles, the share was 41.7% as well; 49.5% of newly built single-family homes in San Francisco were below the threshold; and in San Diego, 61.4% were. Proposition 37 has the potential to help prospective California homebuyers, but only if they can find new homes to buy."

Proposition 45 limits timelines on environmental reviews for some projects under the California Environmental Quality Act. These new requirements would expedite environmental review and court challenges to projects.

Votes for the measure are still being counted. With 65% of the votes counted on Friday, 54.9% of Californians have voted against it while 45.1% voted in favor.

Affordable housing advocates—including the Associated General Contractors of California, the California Building Industry Association, the California Council for Affordable Housing, and the California Chamber of Commerce—expressed support for the measure.

Environmental groups and labor unions widely opposed it, arguing that it would reduce environmental oversight and community decision making around new development.

She previously worked at Business Insider as an executive editor covering news, entertainment, and digital culture, and was the company's founding Singapore bureau chief. She was also a senior editor at BuzzFeed, covering lifestyle, fashion, and beauty. Ocarina Of Time Newcomers Are Getting Killed By Cuccos

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The driving force behind Nintendo wanting to remake The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is to give new fans, some who weren’t even born when the original was released, a modernized version of the game that they’re more likely to fall in love with. As some of those new fans have quickly discovered, that also means learning tougher lessons older fans had to learn 28 years ago.

As much as the way we play games may have changed since the launch of the original Ocarina, there are some things that remain the same. One of those things is that you put a gamer into a virtual space with some hapless chickens, or cuccos since this is Zelda, those chickens are going to get messed with.

We finally got a chance to play The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake and it's still one of the best games ever made.

Cuccos are a staple of the Zelda series, and you encounter them frequently in Ocarina of Time. Veterans know not to attack them, learning the hard way 28 years ago that doing so means they’ll attack you right back. Turns out that detail has been retained for the remake, and streamer IShowSpeed found out about the legendary detail the hard way.

Less than two hours into a livestream of his Ocarina remake playthrough, Speed’s chat coaxed him into attacking a cucco. Upon being struck with Link’s sword, the cucco crows and summons all of its friends, which rain down from the sky, chipping away at your health, just like they did in Ocarina on the N64.

There is no escape, so all Speed can do is panic and run around until his health has been depleted. Turns out that was the first time he died. I know that because he started screaming it at the chat who tricked him into hurting a cucco after the screen faded to black.

Don’t worry, Speed, you’re not the only new Zelda fan playing Ocarina for the first time this week who thought they could attack a cucco without paying for it dearly. Streamer Hika has also shared a clip where she dared to swing her sword at a cucco in Hyrule Castle Town, only to then feel the wrath of all of the avian victim’s feathered friends. “I must be the only one who didn’t know this was happening?” Hika’s tweet reads. No, Hika, turns out you’re not alone.

There are so many people already playing the Ocarina remake that details regarding what has changed and what has remained faithful to the original have quickly been discovered. Cucco attacks have crossed the remake barrier. However, some of the references to other games, including Mario, have been removed as Nintendo didn’t want to disrupt the remake’s immersion.
