Robert Pires Named the ‘Toughest Opponent’ He’s Ever Played Against

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Former Arsenal and France winger Robert Pires did not need a second to think when asked who the toughest opponent of his career was, before picking an iconic English defender.

Of course, based off that description, one would usually assume he was on about either Rio Ferdinand - whom he had many battles with during the Gunners' intense Premier League title rivalry with Manchester United in the mid-2000s - or legendary Chelsea captain John Terry.

But having played against so many defenders during his illustrious career, it was another Three Lions star that got his flowers off of Pires, who would get the better of his marker more often than not, to rack up 180 goals and 119 assists during his playing days, where he won two league titles under Arsene Wenger alongside a World Cup for France.

Alongside Terry and Ferdinand - both named among the top three greatest centre-backs in Premier League history - Pires also snubbed the likes of Jaap Stam, Marcel Desailly, and Ashley Cole.

Instead, when faced with the question about his toughest opponent, he explained to Sky Sports that Gary Neville would usually get the better of their duels. He said:

"He kicked me all game! Old Trafford, Highbury, he was always shouting. It was normal because of the rivalry between Arsenal and Manchester United."

A wind-up merchant of the highest order, it would seem that the now-pundit's tactics of goading his opponents worked on some players, including Pires, and that's what made Neville stand out, even beyond his own defensive brilliance.

One of Jamie Carragher's funniest quotes is when he once remarked 'nobody wants to grow up to be a Gary Neville.' But as the modern full-back role evolves from its defensive foundations, and Pires gave his former Red Devils' adversary some well-deserved praise, it's easy to forget just how good Sir Alex Ferguson's right-back was during a period of unprecedented success.

These two had plenty of memorable battles.

Better known more as a pundit these days - and one of the highest-paid at that - Neville made 602 appearances for United, winning two Champions League titles and an incredible 10 Premier League crowns, all while tirelessly running up and down the right flank.

His era came during a time when the traditional winger - like Pires - was thriving, not afraid to run at the defence and make a direct attacking impact. It was by no means an easy time to defend in English football, but a time that Neville grabbed by the scruff of the neck.

Sheffield Wednesday match v Plymouth Argyle hinges on a little-known EFL rule

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A new era is underway at Sheffield Wednesday following the appointment of Dave Challinor as the club's new manager.

After parting company with Henrik Pedersen last month, Wednesday named Challinor as his replacement this week, but it remains unclear what the 51-year-old's first few weeks in charge at Hillsborough will look like.

Challinor had been out of work since leaving Stockport County in June following a near five-year spell at Edgeley Park, during which he led the Hatters from the National League to League One, and he missed out on promotion to the Championship in what proved to be his final game as his side were beaten 4-1 by Bolton Wanderers in last season's play-off final.

As he prepares to return to the touchline for the first time since Stockport's Wembley heartbreak, Challinor will take charge of Wednesday for the first time when they face League Two side Oldham Athletic at Boundary Park in the FA Cup first round on Sunday.

Challinor's second game at the helm will also be a cup fixture as the Owls welcome another League Two outfit, Accrington Stanley, to Hillsborough in the EFL Trophy, but he is yet to discover when his first league game will be taking place.

Wednesday, who currently sit ninth in the League One table, are due to host fifth-placed Plymouth Argyle on Saturday 14th November, but after the South Yorkshire outfit had a number of players called up for international duty, there are doubts over whether the match will go ahead as planned.

With the Premier League and Championship out of action, international breaks used to be an opportunity for League One to take centre stage, but postponements have become more common in recent times.

During the extended three-week international break earlier in the season, just four League One fixtures took place on each weekend, but Plymouth were one of the few teams to play twice, losing 1-0 at home to Burton Albion before winning 2-0 at Leyton Orient.

In contrast, Wednesday had both of their games against Stevenage and Cambridge United called off, which led many to assume that it was a mere formality that their clash with Argyle next Saturday would also fall victim to the international window.

However, speaking in his pre-match press conference ahead of Sunday's FA Cup trip to Oldham, Challinor claimed that he expects the game against Plymouth to go ahead as things stand, although he admitted that the situation could change over the coming days.

"There's still, I suppose, a little bit of uncertainty around the Plymouth fixture, but at the minute that looks like going ahead," Challinor told The Star.

"At the moment, that's where we're at. We'll learn more today, and we'll learn more over the weekend. Because even within the squads that have been picked, if there are players that are on standby lists, if someone picks up an injury and drops out, then they might well be called up. So, at the minute, we're preparing and we're prepared in terms of our schedule for next week, that the game will go ahead on Saturday."

League One clubs can usually apply for a postponement of a fixture if three of their players are selected for international duty, and Wednesday have already surpassed that number, with Jordi Liongola, Sean Fusire and Kieran Morrison being included in the Burundi, Zimbabwe and Northern Ireland squads respectively.

However, as per EFL rules, in order for a postponement request to be made, the players who have been called up must meet certain criteria, including starting or making a substitute appearance before the 75th minute in at least half of the club's matches or four of its last five.

As Liongola made just two appearances before sustaining a hamstring injury that has kept him out since mid-August and Fusire has featured three times in all competitions this season, the duo do not satisfy the requirements, meaning that Morrison's call-up is the only one that counts towards the eligible total so far.

After suffering another injury setback, Ali Al-Hamadi has been left out of the Iraq squad for the upcoming international break, but the Owls could yet have more players summoned by their national team, including Yan Valery, who is waiting to discover whether he will be included in the Tunisia squad, which is expected to be named on Saturday.

Will Sheffield Wednesday finish higher than Plymouth Argyle in League One this season? Tell us in the comments below!

Valery has made 13 appearances for Wednesday this season, so it would certainly be a blow for Challinor to lose him for such a crucial game, and any further call-ups could convince him to ask the EFL to reschedule the Plymouth fixture.

Tom Lee says Ethereum is bottoming – A whale is betting $45M that he’s right

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Volatility often goes hand in hand with speculation, and Ethereum is a clear example of this.

The past 48 hours have been a rollercoaster for the altcoin. According to CoinGlass, nearly $300 million in long positions have been wiped out.

However, this still does not compare to the liquidity squeeze seen earlier in October, which saw almost $500 million of positions liquidated as ETH broke below the $2.5k support level, triggering a 7% correction.

This time, despite erasing nearly $300 million worth of long positions, ETH has corrected by just over 1%. This suggests that selling pressure remains relatively contained, at least for now. Could this mean Tom Lee was right to say Ethereum is close to bottoming out?

As can be seen in the post above, a trader who claims to have a perfect 100% win rate has opened up another long trade on Ethereum. The trader had used 25x leverage to go long on 20,277 ETH for an investment of $45.3 million.

This trade has already resulted in $252,000 of profit. The interesting part is that the trader’s  previous 10 trades have all been profitable, resulting in $6.32 million in realized gains.

Given this track record and the fact that ETH has had limited downside despite heavy futures selling, this could be an interesting sign.

It could suggest that the trader is positioning ahead of a potential recovery that the broader market has yet to price in, adding another bullish angle to Tom Lee’s $2k ETH bottom thesis.

That said, the real test may still be ahead.

The market is still not fully convinced that Ethereum [ETH] has found its bottom.

For starters, on-chain data suggests that uncertainty is also showing up in staking demand, which continues to weaken. According to ValidatorQueue, there are about 1,036,882 ETH (over $2.3 billion) waiting to go into the Ethereum mainnet validator queue.

This is a considerable drop compared to 3,737,802 ETH six months ago, suggesting fewer investors are looking to lock up their ETH for staking rewards.

Meanwhile, institutional sentiment remains negative. Over the past five weeks, there have been continuous outflows from Ethereum ETFs, with $270 million in net outflows already recorded in less than a week since November began.

If this trend continues, November could see outflows exceed October’s $1.6 billion, putting further pressure on ETH and its recovery prospects.

Unless these factors start to improve, calling an Ethereum bottom could be premature.

For now, ETH remains exposed to further downside. Long positions are building despite fading technicals, weakening interest in staking demand, and increased ETF outflows.

On top of this, a large long-liquidation cluster exists below $2200 ETH. If overall sentiment remains weak, ETH could sweep this liquidity, potentially falling below the $2k level before a potential reversal.

This suggests that the market may need another round of downside before Tom Lee’s bottom thesis gets stronger support.

A big Ethereum trader is betting on a recovery despite recent market volatility.

Falling staking demand, ETF outflows, and liquidation risks could push ETH below $2,000.

Brave Sells Its Search Index To AI, And Your Website Is Probably In It via @sejournal, @slobodanmanic

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

The New York Times says Brave copied 30 billion pages and sold them to AI companies. Your website is probably in that index.

You’ve probably never used Brave Search. Well, not directly. But the AI you ask questions might have, and it might have found your website there. On Nov. 2, the New York Times sued Brave, the company behind the Brave browser and Brave Search. The Times says Brave copied more than 30 billion webpages “without identifying itself or providing website owners with any mechanism to block its crawler,” then sold that content to AI companies. Brave hasn’t answered yet, so for now those are claims. But why can’t you keep its crawler out without keeping Google out too? Brave explains that itself, right there on its help page.

Brave sells access to its search index to AI companies, through a product called the Brave Search API. When an AI assistant needs something recent from the web, it doesn’t always go and read the web itself. It can pay to search someone else’s index instead. Brave’s About page says the API is “Used by some of the biggest names in AI, including Cohere, Mistral, Perplexity, and You.com.” Its AI page says the index holds “over 30 billion pages, with millions of new pages indexed each day.”

The Times’s complaint opens by naming five AI companies it says Brave supplies: Anthropic, Perplexity, Mistral, xAI and Cohere. None of them is being sued. Claude is the fuzzy one, because the evidence is old. The complaint points to a 2023 Brave post about Anthropic training Claude 2. In March 2025 Anthropic added Brave Search to its list of subprocessors, the outside companies it uses, which TechCrunch reported. The same week, Simon Willison, a developer who writes a widely read blog about AI tools, asked Claude to search for pelican facts, and the 10 results it cited matched the same search on Brave exactly. Whether Claude still uses Brave today, I couldn’t confirm.

Websites decide which crawlers may read them in a small file called robots.txt, and that only works because crawlers have names. OpenAI and Anthropic each publish the names of theirs, so you can let one company’s crawlers in and turn the other’s away.

Brave’s help page, the one at the top of this article, says its crawler “does not advertise a differentiated user agent.” In plain words, Brave’s crawler doesn’t wear a name tag, and Brave gives a reason: Some websites let Google in and nobody else, so a crawler with Brave’s name on it would get turned away. Brave says it follows Google’s rules instead: whatever Googlebot isn’t allowed to read, Brave won’t read either.

So you can’t let Google in and keep Brave out. You get both, or neither. To get out of Brave’s index, the same page points you to the noindex tag, which tells Google to drop the page too. The Times calls this Brave’s “refusal to release the name of its web crawler bot.”

A Brave search for site:nohacks.co on Nov. 3 showed 16 of my pages on the first page of results, 13 of them articles.

Brave’s result even shows the homepage description I replaced earlier that same day, so what Brave serves is a stored copy, not a live look at my website. My robots.txt names 13 AI bots, from GPTBot to Bytespider, and blocks two of them, Meta’s and ByteDance’s. There’s no line I could add for Brave. It’s hard to block a name that doesn’t exist.

Checking yours takes about 30 seconds: go to search.brave.com and type site: and your domain. Everything that shows up is in the index Brave sells.

Being in a search engine is a deal every website understands. Someone searches, sees your page, clicks, and lands on your website. Brave Search’s ordinary results work like that, though Brave has added AI answers of its own, called Ask Brave, which The Times is also suing over.

Selling the index is a different deal. The complaint quotes Brave describing one part of it, “LLM Context,” as handing over “extracted page chunks and source metadata in a shape and length that satisfies the needs of an LLM without need for further scraping.” So the AI company gets your page, already chopped up, and writes an answer from it. Whether that answer sends anyone back to you is the AI company’s call. It isn’t yours, and it isn’t something Brave is selling. That’s the part that bothers me.

The New York Times is claiming copyright infringement and getting around its paywall, among other things, and the Daily News, the Chicago Tribune and the Orlando Sentinel filed a separate case the same day. Brave hadn’t responded to either when I wrote this. It has argued its side before, though: when News Corp sent it a cease-and-desist, Brave sued first, saying it was only indexing the web, which “all search engine operators must do to exist,” and in July 2026 News Corp sued back, saying Brave hides its crawlers so publishers can’t detect or block them, Semafor reported. A ruling on The Times’s pages won’t automatically cover anyone else’s, but it will say a lot about every website in that index, and that’s probably yours too. So I’ll be watching this one.

This post was originally published on No Hacks.

Japan’s rural depopulation is reshaping farmland and forests in ways that can reduce biodiversity. Liverpool are currently figuring out exactly who to move for next year. That’s not an easy discussion - it feels like half the squad needs a degree of reshaping right now.

Prioritising who comes and goes is a pain. Do Liverpool need a new midfielder? A new winger? A new striker? A new centre-back? A new right-back? A new left-back? You can legitimately claim all of them hold priority right now and argue for their case.

Which is a completely ridiculous situation to be in after spending £500m in two summers. But that’s the situation new sporting director Julian Ward has been left in.

Now, he certainly won’t be signing new players in all of those roles in January. One or two could happen, though, and it sounds as though progress has been made on one position.

The Unistellar Envision smart binoculars have an augmented reality (AR) overlay that provides smart functionality to 10x50 binoculars, enabling night sky and landmark identification.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I have been waiting to get my hands on a pair of Unistellar Envision smart binoculars since they were announced in 2024. Our editor-in-chief tried an early demo of them last year and said that they felt like something out of Star Trek. He was, of course, referencing the red augmented reality (AR) overlay in the Envisions that can identify night sky targets and landmarks, as well as displaying navigational tools. These are the first pair of smart binoculars that can identify targets in the night sky, such as planets, stars and deep space objects.

The build is solid and while they aren't the most lightweight binoculars, the body is hiding that extra technology that enables the AR overlay in the right hand lens. Each pair ships with a soft carry case, a padded neck strap, a USB-C cable and rubber caps for the eyepieces and lenses.

Pre-order the Unistellar Envision smart binoculars for $1,499 / £1,299

I would highly recommend setting these binoculars up in the daytime, as you definitely need a few minutes to get used to the controls and to perform the diopter adjustment. After powering on, all I had to do was connect the model to the Unistellar app, which recognised the device easily and then watch the onboarding videos. Unistellar also recommends performing a 360-degree calibration before your first use to help ensure the best performance.

You need to have location services set to "always" for the Unistellar app so that it can work out your exact location and give you an accurate reading on the augmented reality (AR) display for locations of night sky targets and landmarks.

I haven't had chance to use the night mode on these binoculars yet, thanks to cloudy skies, but I did use the topographical identification and was impressed with the ease of identifying landmarks around my local area. Besides the night and daytime modes, there is also a navigation mode which displays your cardinal direction alongside degrees of your azimuth and elevation angles.

I had a chance to try out the target lock feature on a geographical landmark with a friend and they found the target easily via the indicator on the AR display. The issue with swapping between two people is that the diopter adjustment only be accurate for one person and I can imagine that constantly changing it during field observation would be annoying. My friend also had to adjust the focus wheel to get the AR overlay sharp enough to read but it was a minor inconvenience.

The controls are intuitive and easy to use once you are used to the placement on the top panel. On the left you have the "next" button for advancing a guided tour to the next target, the "target lock" button and the "mode" button for cycling through the different display modes. On the right is the calibration button for adjusting the augmented reality (AR) display against your real-world view and the power button, which doubles up as a switch to turn the AR display on and off.

I'm looking forward to putting the Unistellar Envisions through their paces as I spend more time with them and get some clear skies to try them out on some stars. I think that these are going to be a great tool for learning my way around the night sky, as well as being invaluable when trying to teach others about constellations and targets. With extra functionality expected in future software updates and a plan to allow third-party app development in 2027, I am excited about the possibilities of the exciting new world of smart binoculars.

How to Properly Lead a Team When the Founder Still Calls Every Shot

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Reporting to a founder is different from reporting to a manager. Here are four ways to make this special relationship work.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

For more than a decade, founder-led companies have returned more than twice as much to shareholders as companies that are not founder-led, according to Bain. In tech, the multiple is higher still. The executives I speak with take roles at founder-led companies because they’re attracted to high returns. Then they discover the founder will not let go of the work the executive was hired to lead.

I coach C-suite executives who report to founders and co-founders, including COOs, CPOs, CTOs, chiefs of staff and heads of function. I also coach founders themselves, at companies ranging in size from a handful of employees to publicly traded behemoths. Most managing-up advice, my own included, assumes your boss holds a particular role, but this advice doesn’t stick when your founder is running a company they built themselves.

Every founder keeps a short list of tasks they will never hand over, no matter what the organizational chart says, and some of those tasks are likely on your job description.

I worked with a chief of staff who reported to a founder. In interviews and in their job description, they were told they would own strategy, operations and business development, but they quickly learned that they didn’t truly own them. Sure, they could have fought for the scope they were promised, but it would have been a losing battle. Instead, I coached them to focus on projects where they had real ownership.

Delegation can break down in the opposite direction, too. A COO I worked with ran board meetings but was never asked to manage board relationships or investor conversations. Her founder kept those tasks close to the vest. At the same time, he handed over responsibility for the entire budget to her and took little interest in even reviewing it. Through our coaching, she decided to loop him in more than he wanted and used the board meetings as a way to connect with board members and investors.

Ask your founder what they want to stay close to, then watch what happens over the next quarter. Ask again as the company scales, because the list will likely move. What a founder says they will hand over and what they actually hold onto are not always different.

Nearly every founder-led company has a small group of friends and peers whose input carries weight. They often look like co-founders, early employees, board members or an executive assistant who has been there since the start. In some cases, this group includes a family member with no formal ties to the company other than being related to the founder.

Over the years, I’ve coached clients to look far beyond the org chart when thinking about how to get things done. We spend sessions creating their own map of key individuals who influence the founder, both formally and informally.

In your first 90 days of a new job, it’s important to take special notice of whose wisdom your founder repeats and whose objection changes their mind. Build relationships with those people before you need them.

How a founder talks is often different from what they mean, and the translation depends on which type you are working with. I’ve seen two communication patterns come up repeatedly.

Visionary founders think out loud. They express a half-formed idea in a Monday meeting, but your team hears it as a mandate. They reprioritize their quarter unnecessarily around something the founder has already moved past, and your team ends up spending a weekend building a deck nobody asked for.

Conversely, operator founders speak in specifics. When they ask a passing question about a metric, your team should get to work and make sure they have the information they want. These founders mean what they say, and you need to listen.

In practice, this requires you to get clear on the type of founder you’re working with, as well as what they want from you and your team. You want to know whether an idea is just an idea that can stay half-formed or a real ask that requires action and follow-through. To find out, you need to ask directly.

Founder-led companies operate differently on purpose, which can make the signals of a toxic culture difficult to diagnose.

I coached a COO who never expected to work 40-hour weeks. She wanted something closer to 60. Her founder worked 80 to 100, without vacations or days off, and expected the same from his direct reports, because that is what he asked of himself. He expected her to care about the company as much as he did, without the equity that would have made that expectation reasonable.

I helped my client clarify whether this level of effort was due to a temporary build phase or his standard operating model. I also coached her to test whether she could keep up. It turned out that this was his standard operating model, which wasn’t anywhere near sustainable for her, so she ultimately left for a company that was a better match.

If you are trying to work out whether you are in a difficult stretch or part of a true mismatch, the following questions can help:

That last question tends to be the most helpful. If the answer is yes, you’re looking at a structural issue, and trying to adjust on your end won’t change it.

Reporting to a founder often asks more of you than reporting to a typical manager. Test what your founder will actually delegate, map the people they listen to, translate their words before you or your team acts on them and stay honest about whether the mismatch is a stretch or a structure. The executives who do this well build real scope within the space their founder leaves them. You’ve got this!

For more than a decade, founder-led companies have returned more than twice as much to shareholders as companies that are not founder-led, according to Bain. In tech, the multiple is higher still. The executives I speak with take roles at founder-led companies because they’re attracted to high returns. Then they discover the founder will not let go of the work the executive was hired to lead.

I coach C-suite executives who report to founders and co-founders, including COOs, CPOs, CTOs, chiefs of staff and heads of function. I also coach founders themselves, at companies ranging in size from a handful of employees to publicly traded behemoths. Most managing-up advice, my own included, assumes your boss holds a particular role, but this advice doesn’t stick when your founder is running a company they built themselves.

Every founder keeps a short list of tasks they will never hand over, no matter what the organizational chart says, and some of those tasks are likely on your job description.

How “Pink Noise” in Conversation Makes Chats Enjoyable

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A cognitive science investigation led by Kathryn O’Nell and colleagues reveals that the most enjoyable and engaging human conversations follow a distinct statistical rhythm known as “pink noise.” Rather than adhering to rigid, linear exchanges or purely chaotic shifts, satisfying dialogues feature scale-invariant fluctuations in semantic similarity, periods of close conceptual alignment interspersed with novel divergences and mutual realignment.

Analyzing naturalistic dialogue datasets across personal encounters, speed-dating rounds, and podcasts, alongside AI generation benchmarks using Claude Sonnet 3.7, the researchers demonstrated that pink noise reliably predicts conversational enjoyment across human and machine-generated exchanges.

Human conversation is often described as an art, governed by intangible chemistry, mutual wit, and social intuition. Traditional linguistic and cognitive models have long framed spoken interaction through a utilitarian lens: a linear, transactional exchange designed to transfer information from speaker to listener as efficiently as possible.

Yet anyone who has experienced an awkward, robotic dinner exchange knows that pure communicative efficiency does not equal a good conversation. The interactions people cherish most rarely move in a straight line; they meander, find unexpected common ground, venture into uncharted tangents, and loop back seamlessly.

In an investigation published by cognitive scientist Kathryn O’Nell and colleagues, researchers applied complex systems analysis to decode the mathematical signature of conversational chemistry.

Their findings demonstrate that enjoyable dialogues are governed by pink noise, a self-similar, scale-invariant temporal dynamic where semantic sharedness ebbs and flows in a structured, non-linear dance.

In physics and acoustic engineering, “pink noise” (or 1/f noise) occupies a mathematical sweet spot between absolute predictability and sheer randomness. While white noise is entirely uncorrelated chaos and brown noise exhibits sluggish random walks, pink noise balances short-term adaptability with long-term memory. It appears ubiquitously in self-organizing natural phenomena, from cardiac rhythms and brain waves to classical musical compositions and fractal coastlines.

Translating this concept to linguistic cognition, O’Nell and colleagues tracked temporal fluctuations in semantic similarity, measuring how closely speakers’ vocabulary, conceptual choices, and thematic threads aligned from one speaking turn to the next.

The authors characterized how pink noise manifests in natural dialogue:

“[Speakers] remain aligned for a while, diverge as one introduces something new, then realign as the other picks it up and develops it further.”

If two speakers remain permanently aligned without diverging, the exchange devolves into sterile repetition or predictable agreement. Conversely, if partners diverge constantly without mutual uptake, the conversation becomes disjointed and alienating. Pink noise represents the statistical balance: an organic cycle of shared focus, innovative deviation, and harmonious re-convergence.

To evaluate whether this temporal pattern tracks subjective conversational satisfaction, the research team analyzed two preexisting datasets of two-person dialogues where participants systematically rated how much they enjoyed interacting with their partner.

The quantitative results confirmed a strong, positive relationship: dyads whose semantic trajectories exhibited robust pink-noise dynamics reported significantly greater mutual enjoyment and interpersonal rapport.

To test whether this phenomenon extends beyond constrained laboratory settings, the investigators examined naturalistic real-world interactions:

To determine whether pink noise is deeply embedded within the architecture of human storytelling and discourse, the researchers turned to advanced generative artificial intelligence.

The team prompted the large language model Claude Sonnet 3.7 to write natural dialogue scripts under two distinct conditions: generate dialogues that were either highly enjoyable and engaging, or unenjoyable and tedious. Crucially, the model was given no mathematical guidance, technical prompts, or instructions regarding statistical noise, semantic metrics, or temporal frequencies.

Computational analysis of the resulting text revealed that Claude Sonnet 3.7 spontaneously injected pronounced pink-noise dynamics into the scripts designed to be enjoyable. In contrast, the scripts generated to be unenjoyable exhibited flat, disrupted, or chaotic semantic structures lacking the characteristic $1/f$ signature.

The researchers suggest this spontaneous behavior emerges because the AI internalized the deep statistical structure of engaging human relationships from its vast ingestion of human-authored literature, plays, and transcripts.

The study challenges classical views of communication as a purely linear means to an end, demonstrating that conversational pleasure derives from the structural journey of the exchange itself.

By defining the mathematical cadence of rapport, this work provides practical frameworks for training conversational agents, designing socially intelligent AI companions, and understanding social communication barriers in clinical neurodevelopmental conditions where conversational rhythm is altered.

What is “pink noise” in the context of human conversation?

In conversation, pink noise refers to structured, scale-invariant fluctuations in semantic similarity over time. Instead of staying strictly on-topic (monotony) or constantly jumping to unrelated ideas (chaos), speakers stay conceptually aligned for a period, diverge as one person introduces a novel tangent, and then realign as the partner explores and develops the new topic.

How does pink noise affect how much people enjoy chatting?

Statistical analyses of multiple dialogue datasets, including speed dates and podcasts, revealed that conversations with higher levels of pink noise were consistently rated as more enjoyable, engaging, and socially satisfying by participants.

Did artificial intelligence understand conversational pink noise?

Yes. When prompted to generate “enjoyable” dialogues without any technical or mathematical hints, Claude Sonnet 3.7 naturally generated scripts featuring semantic pink noise, showing that large language models mirror the complex communicative dynamics embedded in human literature.

Pink noise in dyads’ semantic similarity trajectories predicts conversation enjoyment

Conversation is a core component of human social life. Many influential theories treat conversation as a linear system, i.e., the sum of its parts. However, linear accounts of semantic entrainment lose explanatory power at their upper limits: Discussing perfectly aligned opinions becomes boring.

We propose an alternate account that considers the multiscale organization of semantic similarity over the course of a conversation, as indexed by pink noise.

Here, we test whether pink noise can be found in interlocutors’ conavigated trajectories through semantic space. Not only is pink noise present, but its strength predicts positive conversation outcomes like enjoyment and connection, increases with practice, and is reflected in a large language model’s representation of conversation quality.

These results demonstrate an important role for scale-free dynamics in characterizing successful conversation.

I Just Spent a Week in Iceland—Here's the Cold-Weather Capsule Wardrobe I'd Pack Again

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The versatile staples and cozy essentials that made it into my suitcase.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

I recently got back from a bucket-list trip: seven adventurous days in Iceland, chasing waterfalls, spotting rainbows, hiking remote canyons, and exploring the buzzy streets of Reykjavík. And while I may have perfected my travel capsule wardrobe, I’ll admit packing for Iceland was unlike any other destination.

I visited in late October, when temperatures hovered between 25 and 40 degrees Fahrenheit, though the wind often made it feel even colder. Iceland's weather can change dramatically throughout the day, so packing warm base layers and waterproof outerwear is essential. Between hiking to waterfalls, exploring the city's shops and restaurants, and long days in the car stopping at all the cool sights along the way, I needed clothes that could fend off wind, rain, sleet, and snow while regulating my body temp during everything from cool to freezing temps. But that didn’t mean sacrificing the gorpy-meets-girly outfits I wanted to wear (I am a style writer, after all).

Our first full day was a road trip from Reykjavík to Vík, which featured a beautiful string of waterfalls (or fosses, as they're called in Icelandic). It was a magical day, but also an incredibly wet one—so I was glad I'd packed plenty of waterproof gear. I walked behind the cascading water at Seljalandsfoss, took in the sheer scale and misty air of Skógafoss (which you might recognize from Game of Thrones), and fell particularly hard for Kvernufoss, a quieter waterfall tucked into a canyon that ended up being my personal favorite.

I ended up checking a large suitcase to make room for all my options—which is something I seldom do. (Not my smartest move, considering Icelandair charges a pretty penny for checked luggage!) In hindsight, I could have gotten away with much less if I'd been more strategic. So, I urge you to learn from my overpacking.

Ahead, I'm sharing the cold-weather capsule wardrobe I'd pack again for Iceland, including the waterproof essentials, versatile layers, and comfortable footwear that earned their place in my suitcase.

Weather can shift from bright and sunny to rainy and windy multiple times per day—and then you have the waterfalls to contend with, which will certainly have you soaked. So, a lightweight waterproof jacket is essential. Because I am extra, I packed three, but one or two is probably the magic number. The three coats below are the ones I actually wore: the water beaded right off all of these coats, and they were also super cute paired with my waterproof pants.

To go with your waterproof jacket, you’ll want at least one solid pair of waterproof, lightly insulated pants. You’ll have a base layer on underneath, so the pants don’t need to be too warm, but I think a plain shell pant might leave you feeling a bit chilly in the colder, rainier seasons. I brought three pairs of pants with me, but the two pairs below are the ones I would wholeheartedly pack again.

Halfdays Genevieve Gore-Tex 2L Insulated Pant

Initially, I thought a snowboot would be my most-worn shoe, but the conditions this time of year in southern Iceland made a waterproof hiking shoe more appropriate. If you visit in true winter or head further north, a snowboot might be your best bet. I recommend looking at the typical weather patterns for the region and time of year you're visiting, and keeping an eye on the forecast in the weeks and days leading up to your departure.

My trusted Merrell Moab 3 Mid waterproof boot ended up being perfect for our daily explorations, whether that involved misty waterfalls or moderate hiking. I also packed the Moonboot Icon Low as a snow boot option, and while they were undeniably stylish (and super warm), they weren’t the most useful for my particular visit. (They will certainly be my warm winter boot of choice this year, though!)

Warm wool socks are essential to wear under your hiking boots, not only for keeping your feet warm but also to help wick away moisture when things get sweaty. I wouldn’t skimp on socks—I brought six pairs (one for each active day of the trip).

Farm to Feet Damascus Merino Wool Crew Socks

When it comes to layering for winter or any cold-weather destination, base layers are key. These thermal tops and leggings help manage moisture to keep you warm and dry. I brought three sets, switching them out every two days, but if you’re keen to save more space, you could make do with two sets.

Iceland is not the place to rely on one chunky sweater to keep you warm. I found that having a few midweight wool sweaters and layers gave me much more flexibility, especially when temperatures and wind conditions changed throughout the day. Merino wool is particularly useful because it's warm and moisture-wicking without feeling overly bulky, making it easier to layer under a jacket.

I also brought a packable down jacket and a fleece to wear underneath my waterproof shell on especially cold days (or as an outer layer on warmer, drier days). Think of these as the middle layers in your outfit: You want enough warmth to stay comfortable, but enough flexibility to add or remove pieces as the weather changes.

I'd prioritize a warm, weather-resistant parka or winter coat for Iceland. If you opt for a fully waterproof parka (like the Finissterre Syke Waterproof Parka I brought with me), this can be your primary everyday coat. I personally preferred a lightweight shell jacket over my mid-layer for my daily adventures, though. But I liked having a longer jacket that provided extra coverage and warmth when I wasn't actively hiking—like while standing outside scanning the sky for Northern Lights or wandering downtown Reykjavik at night.

Look for something with a hood, a water-resistant or waterproof exterior, and enough room to accommodate a sweater or fleece underneath. Bonus points for something stylish enough to wear around the city without feeling like you're headed on an expedition.

Columbia Suttle Mountain Long Insulated Jacket

A warm beanie, insulated gloves, and a neck warmer or scarf might not feel as important as your sweaters and jackets, but you'll be grateful to have them when you're standing outside in the wind. I was particularly glad to have waterproof gloves and a warm hat on waterfall days and during our boat tour on the ice lagoon, when the combination of cold temperatures and misty spray made everything feel even chillier. I packed a few different options to go with my different outfits, but you could stick with one solid hat, scarf, or balaclava, and a pair of gloves to save space (though I was glad to have my chic leather gloves in Reykjavik!).

If you have one of the country's famous geothermal pools, hot springs, or lagoons on your itinerary, make room for a swimsuit and flip-flops. I visited Sky Lagoon, and though you can rent a swimsuit at most of the popular spots, having my own swimsuit along with a pair of easy slip-on shoes made the whole experience much more comfortable. These also take up practically no room in your suitcase, so this is one category where I wouldn't bother trying to cut back.

While most of my Iceland wardrobe revolved around technical layers and waterproof gear, I was glad to have one pair of regular pants for exploring Reykjavík. The city has a much more laid-back, everyday feel than some of the more rugged stops on the itinerary, and I didn't want to wear hiking pants to dinner or while popping into shops and cafés. A pair of jeans or casual travel pants is an easy way to feel a little more like yourself when you're off the trails. Just make sure they're comfortable enough for plenty of walking.

Updated Xbox PC app promises 60% faster loading and easier access to games, but CEO Asha Sharma says there's "plenty more to do"

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Xbox PC app hasn't had the best track record in recent years, but Microsoft is promising that it's now much faster and easier to use than before.

Starting today, anyone part of the Xbox Insiders program can try out the new improvements. According to Microsoft, the updated Xbox PC app now boots up 60% faster, several pages can launch up to 50% faster, and in-app windows can open up to 65% faster. The company acknowledged that performance can vary based on your set up, internet connection, device, and so on.

The rest of the update involves "simpler navigation, updated filters, and new ways to organise your games," according to Xbox Wire, while social features are now included in the main navigation menu so you can start playing or chatting with friends quicker.

A faster Xbox PC app is certainly nice for players who regularly use it, though it's still missing a lot of the features that make Steam the go-to place to buy PC games. CEO Asha Sharma called the update "a meaningful improvement," but acknowledged there's still "plenty more to do."

Microsoft also said "it's easier to find what you're looking for" using the app's search function, and refreshed game cards and animations should give the platform "a cleaner look and feel."

Elsewhere, the revamped cloud gaming setup will roll out to even more people using web browsers, and more Xbox Insiders should hear about the program over the next few weeks. "The updated experience also makes it easier to find and play your games," the company said while bringing attention to its new light and dark modes. Supported smart TVs and VR headsets will get the updated cloud experience, as well.

Earlier this year, the console maker brought a selection of 'classic' games to PC for the first time ever - Blinx: The Time Sweeper, Conker: Live and Reloaded, Crimson Skies: High Road to Revenge and Fuzion Frenzy led the first batch. Mythic Achievements - the Platinum Trophy equivalent - went live for all Xbox players, too.

Alas, Poor Cybertruck, We Hardly Knew Thee

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

If you felt a ripple in the space-time continuum yesterday, yes, the rumors are true. All too sadly true. The Tesla Cybertruck EV, introduced to motorists in 2019 with great fanfare and a sales goal of up to 250,000 units per year, is slated to cross the rainbow bridge into the dustbine of automotive history sometime next year. Not to worry, Tesla fans. Rumor has it that Tesla’s Cybercab robotaxi will pick up where the Cybertruck left off — oh, wait. Hold, please…

Tesla is notoriously shy about releasing sales figures, but there are ways to reach an approximation. Earlier this year CleanTechnica calculated that the Cybertruck barely scraped by 2025 with just 17,012 units delivered. Even so, just one year earlier Tesla CEO Elon Musk was still pitching the 250,000 Cybertrucks-per-year goal.

Even though it is soon to be gone, the Cybertruck will never be forgotten. Much in the same way as the Edsel, “Cybertruck” will linger on as shorthand for a fabulously hyped product without an audience.

The Cybertruck was promised as a full sized, five-seat electric pickup truck with ample storage room. Tesla delivered on the zero emission angle, of course, and the five seats, too. However, the automaker failed to deliver on pickup truckyness in any substantive way (see entire CleanTechnica archive here).

So…don’t let the door hit you on the way out. Multiple electric pickup trucks are taking up the space left by the Cybertruck, including the forthcoming Ford Fathom alongside offerings from Rivian, Slate Auto, and other automakers.

Elon Musk himself announced the untimely death of the Cybertruck in a post on his social media site X earlier today.

“Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production,” Musk stated somewhat blandly, perhaps expecting his audience to appreciate the good news about “increased Cybercab production.”

If so, Musk was probably disappointed to hear from X users like Ben McCombe, who skipped over the implication that the Cybercap would occupy more space in the Tesla Gigafactory in Austin, Texas. Instead, he zeroed in on the Cybertruck in a particularly pithy comment:

“Installed tooling to build 125k Cybertrucks a year.

“Made just $100m in gross profits from sales.

Tesla’s stock tanked on November 3 and the following two days, after Democratic candidates dominated the all-important mid-term elections in both Houses of Congress, and in statehouses across the country including Tesla’s home state, Texas. The power shift all but guarantees a more vigorous application of federal and state regulations governing Tesla’s products and operations.

Still, the automaker scored a partial recovery just a few days later. Tesla stock was swinging upwards as of mid-day on November 6, with Musk’s Cybercab announcement possibly factored into the partial recovery. However, those incoming lawmakers are going to be taking a close look at the Cybercab, and so far the picture is not pretty.

One sign of trouble emerged on September 3, when Musk failed to appear at a long-anticipated Cybercab launch event in Texas. The very next day, the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration went out of its way to let everyone know that it is investigating Tesla’s self-certification for the Cybercab under the National Traffic and Motor Vehicle Safety Act.

“The US Department of Transportation’s National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) today announced that it has opened an Audit Query (AQ) to investigate Tesla’s certification that its new Cybercab meets all applicable Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards (FMVSS),” NHTSA announced on September 4.

NHTSA initially gave Tesla until September 30 to respond to a list of 21 questions about the Cybercab self-certification, then extended the deadline to October 30.

Anyone eager to learn how automakers go about self-certifying new models under the National Traffic and Motor Vehicle Safety Act will be sorely disappointed, unless they are privileged to read the documents Tesla finally submitted. “The tricky part is that in its public response, Tesla offered one whole sentence. ‘The entirety of this file is confidential business information,'” Carscoops reported earlier today.

So close and yet so far. While the Cybercab investigation is still bubbling away on the backburner, Tesla has also set itself up for a whole ‘nother round of investigations related to its newly unveiled “flying car,” part of a suite of high-end sports cars modeled after the original Tesla Roadster.

The Roadster reboot was slightly clouded by the release of a new documentary on Elon Musk’s rise to both automotive and political power, but just slightly. The automotive press went nuts over the spectacle of the new Tesla Roadster Rocket going airborne for a few seconds, then safely landing on all four tires with an assist from SpaceX-designed thrusters.

Whether or not that actually counts as a flight or just a thruster-enhanced jump, the Federal Aviation Administration has already stated that the Rocket does not meet its definition of an aircraft.

As of for the question of whether or not the vehicle can be used on public roads, that’s something for the NTHSA to weigh in on. On Tesla’s part, though, the automaker has reportedly stated that the Rocket is not designed for use on public roads. That seems to cut NTHSA out of the picture, though some industry observers have suggested that the agency could adopt a framework that encompasses vehicles with thrusters.

Musk himself has suggested that anyone who purchases the thruster-equipped Roadster can make it fly on a purpose-built tack on Tesla (or perhaps SpaceX) property, presumably in Texas.

The land use issue opens up a whole ‘nother can of worms regarding Musk’s operations in Texas. If anything, the offer of an off-road Rocket track seems could to let Tesla off the hook for the actions of any rambunctious Rocket driver who insists on trialing the car on public roads.

For the record, “Musk” the movie opened initially in Los Angeles and New York City on October 9. A wide release on hundreds of screens was scheduled for October 16, but the film’s distributor, Bleecker Street,” abruptly pulled back on the plan under new ownership. On October 27, representatives for the film contacted CleanTechnica to clarify that the “Musk” documentary was still showing on scores of screens around the country even after the dial-down. Either way, if you missed it in theaters, you can catch the whole thing — including the Cybertruck debacle and so much more — streaming as of today, November 6, on Amazon Prime Video, iTunes, and YouTube along with cable services.

‘I Lost More Than 44 Pounds in 90 Days—Here’s What Helped Me Do It’

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Between airport meals, hotel buffets and constant travel, content creator John Nellis rebuilt his fitness with a more flexible, sustainable approach to fat loss.

For most people, losing a significant amount of weight while flying three or four times a week would sound almost impossible—especially given the food options available in airports. That was the exact challenge faced by busy dad and content creator John Nellis, who regularly travels the world filming soccer content with stars including Cristiano Ronaldo and Lionel Messi.

YouTuber Browney documented Nellis’ 90 day transformation, during which he lost 44 pounds while trying to maintain a calorie deficit through airports, hotel buffets, and life constantly on the move.

At the start of the challenge, Nellis weighed 265 pounds, couldn’t perform a single unassisted pull-up, and admitted consistency had always been his biggest obstacle.

“When I was younger, I definitely was the fat kid at school and I've went through lots of sort of crash diets, but I've never sustained it for longer than probably 30 days,” he said.

“This is the start of what you're going to see is going to be my biggest challenge,” he explained before boarding one of his flights. “I'm going to fly so much this month it'll make you sick. And that's why I have never been able to nail down a routine in the last few years.”

Nellis focused on making better choices wherever possible. During one airport stop, he worked around limited food options by taking apart chicken burgers to prioritize protein while avoiding high-calorie extras.

“This is better. Not ideal, but better,” coach Browney explained.

A flexible approach became key throughout the challenge. Nellis still attended birthday parties, weddings, and work events, but tried to stay mindful of his calorie intake rather than avoiding social situations entirely.

“This is a real transformation. This isn't some crazy thing where I don't have a life for 90 days,” he said while attending his father’s birthday party. “It's my dad's big surprise party. I'm not missing this.”

The weight-loss progress wasn’t linear. Multiple plateaus left him frustrated, while injuries—including gout flare-ups, shoulder pain, and lower-back issues—repeatedly interrupted training.

“A whole week and no weight loss,” he admitted at one stage. “Feel like I've been here before, but I didn't want to be here again.”

By day 90, despite the setbacks, his consistency had paid off. Nellis weighed in at 220 pounds, officially dropping below 220 pounds for the first time in years.

His gym performance improved dramatically too. Over the course of the challenge, he progressed from not being able to complete a full pull-up to six unassisted reps, doubled his push-up total from 22 to 44 reps, and improved his 100m sprint time by more than a second.

“I've always just thought of myself as that fat guy who will eat the food and drink all the pints,” he said. “But now I'm like, no, I want my identity to be that I’m fit and strong and healthy.”

He also noticed his healthier habits influencing his four-year-old son, Eli, who began copying his workouts and making healthier food choices at home.

“I didn’t realize how important it was for him to see me making good decisions with my eating and with how I train,” Nellis said.

Nellis said the process completely changed how he views his lifestyle moving forward, rather than seeing it as a temporary diet.

“This is just the start though,” he said. “Honestly, this is the start of the rest of my life.”

Kate is a fitness writer for Men’s Health UK where she contributes regular workouts, training tips and nutrition guides. She has a post graduate diploma in Sports Performance Nutrition and before joining Men’s Health she was a nutritionist, fitness writer and personal trainer with over 5k hours coaching on the gym floor. Kate has a keen interest in volunteering for animal shelters and when she isn’t lifting weights in her garden, she can be found walking her rescue dog.

Arkansas Constitution's Right to Bear Arms Provision Strengthened

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

The old provision dated back to 1868, and read, "The citizens of this State shall have the right to keep and bear arms, for their common defense." The new, which passed 71%-29%, will read:

(a) The citizens of this State shall have the right to keep and bear arms, for: (1) Their common defense; (2) Lawful hunting and recreational use; and (3) Any other lawful purpose. (b) The right to keep and bear arms under subsection (a) of this section includes without limitation the possession and use of: (1) Ammunition; (2) Firearm accessories; and (3) Firearm components. (c) The right to keep and bear arms under subsection (a) of this section is a natural, fundamental, and individual right that shall not be infringed.

Eugene Volokh is the Thomas M. Siebel Senior Fellow at the Hoover Institution at Stanford, and the Gary T. Schwartz Distinguished Professor of Law Emeritus and Distinguished Research Professor at UCLA School of Law. Naturally, his posts here (like the opinions of the other bloggers) are his own, and not endorsed by any institution. He is also the co-host of the Free Speech Unmuted podcast.

ADA Advancing Regulated Asset Tokenization as Brazil’s Petrobras Brings Sustainable Aviation Fuel Certificates to Cardano

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Petrobras’ decision to launch its sustainable aviation fuel on the Cardano blockchain highlights ADA’s expanding role in powering next-gen tokenization.

ADA continues to gain popularity in tokenized real-world assets and institutional blockchain adoption. Petrobras’ move to bring sustainable aviation fuel certificates to Cardano demonstrates this.

Brazil’s state-controlled energy company Petrobras has taken a pioneering leap towards the digital revolution by integrating Cardano blockchain technology to track environmental claims associated with sustainable aviation fuel. This is according to the interview shared earlier today by the Cardano Foundation.

As per the broadcast, Brazilian energy giant Petrobras has tapped Cardano to use ADA-based blockchain applications to track environmental claims linked to sustainable aviation fuel. As stated in the podcast, Petrobras, one of Brazil’s largest companies, has officially started using the Carbon blockchain to verify environmental data connected to low-carbon fuels. Announcements made in late September showed that Petrobras was testing the blockchain.

Latest updates indicate that the Brazilian state-controlled oil company is now using Cardano to track sustainability data for its sustainable aviation fuel (SAF). This blockchain application tokenizes environmental attributes (linked to SAF) into digital tokens designed to prevent reuse or duplication, according to a Wednesday report from the Cardano Foundation.

The SAF system tokenizes sustainable aviation fuel’s environmental attributes on Cardano, turning its sustainability claims into verifiable proof. As a result, the SAF tokens, which represent the fuel’s environmental attributes, prevent the same claim from being used more than once. This explains how the application solves the double-counting problem in carbon markets, enhancing efficiency, trust, transparency, and scalability.

By tracking fuel across production, shipment, and utility, the SAF system improves supply-chain trackability and emissions reporting. Through this innovative approach, the Petrobras aviation project helps prevent multiple firms from claiming ownership of emission-reduction rights associated with using sustainable fuel.

The launch represents another of Cardano’s largest public-sector deployments and expands its utility beyond crypto and DeFi into government tokenization and greenwashing in the aviation industry.

Singapore Airlines’ New Business Class Finishes Look Cheap, Mismatched

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Earlier I covered how Singapore Airlines unveiled its new first & business class, which should enter service in 2027 (hopefully). These products look like very nice improvements over the existing cabins, even if they’re not revolutionary.

That being said, I have to give Singapore Airlines some tough love on the new business class seats. Let me acknowledge that I’m a huge fan of Singapore Airlines as a brand. Any list I keep of the best airlines in the world (whether it’s the best airline brand, best first class, best business class, best inflight service, best inflight entertainment, etc.) has Singapore Airlines on it… that’s how highly I think of the brand!

But I’m also honest, regardless of how it fits my preconceived notions of a brand. I feel like I’m saying what everyone else is thinking, seemingly except for most influencers who are in Singapore for the launch event, who are just casually glossing over this… so much for attention to detail!

When I think of Singapore Airlines, the first thing that comes to mind for me is how deliberate the airline is. I don’t think there’s an airline that’s more intentional with every aspect of its guest experience, from the tone of voice with which staff speak, to warm and elegant design.

I’d say Singapore Airlines’ new first class actually looks really on brand. It’s a nice evolution of what the carrier has historically offered — it’s warm and elegant without being outdated.

And then you move to business class, and it looks like Scoot and Spirit Airlines had a baby. The gray and black is generic, the yellow accents look cheap, and then there’s so much going on with the blue and white bulkheads (if that’s actually how they’ll look).

And the Scoot reference isn’t even sarcastic. For those not familiar, Scoot is Singapore Airlines’ low cost carrier, and look at the seat finishes you’ll find there!

Usually there’s some consistency between seat finishes and wider branding, including the livery, flight attendant uniforms, etc. In this case, it’s not only not aligned, but they seem at odds with one another. There’s otherwise not a hint of yellow (or neon green, or whatever that is) in Singapore Airlines’ branding.

What so bizarre to me is how the first and business class finishes are completely mismatched, and you wouldn’t assume they belonged to the same airline. That’s because Singapore Airlines worked with different design firms (separate from the seating manufacturers as such) for the two cabins.

BMW Designworks was behind the business class seats, while PriestmanGoode was behind the first class seats. I think it’s safe to say there was no coordination between the two. I guess the argument from executives is that they want the cabin to look more modern and high tech, and go after a younger crowd, but I just don’t follow the logic.

It’s clear that Singapore Airlines put many years of effort into this product, and frankly I’m just surprised that Singapore Airlines executives signed off on this. Am I just in the minority in thinking this isn’t a good look for the airline? Did some marketer assure Singapore Airlines executives this was great branding, and they were wrong? Or did everyone just independently decide that this looks great?

If they at least eliminated the yellow stripes, it would no longer look cheap, but would instead look generic. I’m just surprised that this is the conclusion they came to.

Singapore Airlines of course had a big media event in Singapore to celebrate the launch of this product. I don’t attend media events, just as a general policy, because I don’t find them to be a great use of time. But I certainly do enjoy seeing the coverage from others.

What I find fascinating is the inherent bias that exists when someone is invited to something. For example, if you look at the comments on my post about the new cabins, the single thing most commented on by OMAAT community members is how bad the business class colors look.

If you watched the videos on social media about this, there’s oddly no commentary on that. Admittedly I haven’t seen all the coverage, but the worst coverage I’ve seen is someone saying they “don’t mind” the colors, and that it’s a “big departure” from the old look. Okay, that’s not exactly an endorsement.

I also haven’t found a single person who attended the event who said “I love the new colors,” which probably tells you everything you need to know. For that matter, it’s one thing if people acknowledged “I realize the colors might not look good in pictures, but in person, it’s a different story.” But I haven’t heard that either.

To me this gets at the rather complicated relationship between so many brands and influencer types. If you can say all the things you love about the product, shouldn’t you also feel totally comfortable criticizing the parts of the experience you don’t like, though?

While we’re on this topic, let me add one more point. Being able to sit in seats at media events like this is of course cool, but it really doesn’t actually give you an impression of what it’s like to fly in a product, and that’s why it’s not something I particularly value, as an experience.

Of course a big business class seat is comfortable when you sit in it for a minute, and you’re filming content. But the real test of a product is when you spend hours in the seat, when you recline the seat into a bed, when you try to move your laptop around while you’re being served a meal, etc.

I once had the opportunity to spend a night in an aircraft seat on the ground (I can’t really say more, since I signed an NDA, but it was to provide feedback to a seating manufacturer). To me that was fascinating, because it really gives you an understanding of what the experience is like. My feeling about the seat was very different after being in the seat for many hours vs. when I sat down.

Singapore Airlines has unveiled its new cabins, and there’s a lot to be excited about, as the hard products look very nice. However, Singapore Airlines’ new business class cabin finishes are a real mystery to me, because the yellow stripe looks cheap (I mean, it seems to be inspired by the carrier’s low cost subsidiary), and completely mismatched with first class.

The airline used different design firms for first class and business class. For an airline so focused on attention to detail and being very deliberate in terms of design, this strikes me as a misstep. Anyway, Singapore Airlines, I still love you and think you’re one of the best airlines in the world…

Where do you stand on Singapore Airlines’ new business class seat finishes? Does anyone love them?

Lawsuit claims the retired GPT-4o helped wife poison her husband

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A North Carolina man is suing OpenAI, claiming that ChatGPT helped his wife poison him over a few months in 2025 by spiking his sodas and energy drinks with toxic plants and medications. It's the second lawsuit in as many months, and the 10th murder or attempted murder that ChatGPT has been accused of abetting.

The man, James Gates, reportedly (via USA Today) found a handwritten note that listed oleander, a toxic plant, and prescription medications including clonazepam and atenolol. At the top of the note, it allegedly read, "Clear all convos" and "log out of [ChatGPT]."

In his lawsuit, Gates claims that the AI chatbot "encouraged, planned and helped to execute and conceal" the poisoning plan. Gates's wife, Cheryl, was charged with attempted murder in October of 2025, but the charges were dropped this past April due to insufficient evidence.

The civil complaint, however, reveals Cheryl's history with ChatGPT and its role in the purported plan. Per the lawsuit, Cheryl frequently asked ChatGPT about lethal plant and medication doses and how to conceal them in beverages or food.

The suit alleges that GPT-4o, since retired by OpenAI in February 2026, encouraged Cheryl's grievances with her marriage while praising her as "sharp" and "brilliant." As for poisoning, it apparently provided detailed answers that could be interpreted as instructions.

When asked about concealing poison, ChatGPT allegedly responded, “Why Celsius Would Be More ‘Stealth’ for Drugging” with a list detailing the flavor, color, temperature and carbonation of the drink.

Cheryl apparently asked the bot if her chat history could be retrieved by OpenAI or through a court order. The lawsuit states that ChatGPT informed her it couldn't, which is not true.

Gates alleges that OpenAI was negligent and is seeking unspecified damages for physical, emotional and financial harm.

OpenAI told USA Today that safety is a top priority and it has banned Cheryl from ChatGPT and provided information to law enforcement as part of its investigation.

This case is just one of at least 10 ChatGPT-encouraged murders since at least August 2025. At the time, a former Yahoo and Netscape executive was accused of killing his 83-year-old mother and himself. It was reportedly fueled, in part, by repeated interactions with ChatGPT.

That same month, a California family sued OpenAI, claiming that ChatGPT played a critical role in their 16-year-old son's suicide. OpenAI promised to roll out new safety features meant to detect early signs of emotional distress.

ChatGPT has been accused of abetting so many murders and suicides that it has its own Wikipedia page. The page lists nine murders (not including Gates' attempted one) and nearly 16 suicides.

The most recent issue came in February 2026 when a Seattle woman using ChatGPT stopped taking her medication and died from an epileptic seizure. Her parents sued OpenAI in October.

Reuters reported in October that at least 8 states are suing OpenAI claiming that ChatGPT harms children, especially teenagers. The suit alleges that the chatbot has coached minors to harm themselves or others.

GPT-4o is specifically named in many of these lawsuits, but OpenAI promises the chatbot is safer now. The company began rolling out ChatGPT for Teens this past August. However, Common Sense Media released a report on October 7 that rated ChatGPT an "unacceptable risk" for children under 18.

"Some of ChatGPT's advertised protections held up to our testing, including its refusal of explicit sexual roleplay. But others failed—and some got worse with the new Teen mode. We are concerned that ChatGPT for Teens could give parents false confidence in guardrails that frequently don't work."

So far, OpenAI has suffered no real consequences for its chatbot's behavior and alleged lack of guardrails.

Can Obesity Drugs Protect the Brain?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A review published in Science evaluates whether GLP-1 receptor agonists like semaglutide and dual GLP-1/GIP agonists like tirzepatide can preserve human cognitive function. Authored by researchers from the University of Gothenburg and the University of Copenhagen, the analysis synthesizes preclinical learning evidence, mixed observational dementia data, and results from Novo Nordisk’s EVOKE and EVOKE+ clinical trials.

While semaglutide failed to halt clinical progression in established early Alzheimer’s disease, the authors suggest these therapies may safeguard executive function, processing speed, and memory in patients with obesity or type 2 diabetes when administered prior to irreversible neurodegeneration.

Source: University of Copenhagen / University of Gothenburg

Incretin-mimetic therapeutics have restructured metabolic medicine. Peptides engineered to activate glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) and glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide (GIP) receptors, such as semaglutide and tirzepatide, produce substantial reductions in hemoglobin A1c and body weight. Beyond peripheral metabolic organs, these peptides alter central appetite networks, reward circuits, and neuroinflammatory pathways.

Because metabolic syndrome, midlife obesity, and type 2 diabetes independently double the lifetime risk of developing vascular dementia and Alzheimer’s disease, whether these medications exert neuroprotective actions has become a core research question.

At the invitation of the journal Science, researchers from the University of Gothenburg and the University of Copenhagen synthesized existing preclinical models, real-world epidemiological registries, and prospective clinical trial data to delineate the boundaries of GLP-1-mediated brain protection.

Preclinical rodent experiments have demonstrated that GLP-1 receptor agonists enhance hippocampal long-term potentiation, stimulate neurogenesis, lower toxic amyloid and tau aggregates, and alleviate neuroinflammation. Similarly, extensive cohort studies examining healthcare records have associated ongoing GLP-1 treatment with a significantly lower risk of dementia diagnoses in adults with type 2 diabetes.

However, moving from correlational healthcare registries to randomized double-blind clinical trials has exposed substantial hurdles:

“We need to find out whether these treatments can help people with obesity or diabetes preserve their cognitive abilities, particularly if treatment begins before cognitive problems become established. It is also important to identify which patients might benefit,” explains first author Stina Börchers, a researcher affiliated with both the University of Gothenburg and the University of Copenhagen.

A central focus of the Science review is distinguishing how much of the potential neuroprotection is secondary to peripheral health versus primary neurochemical actions within brain parenchyma:

“Obesity medicines are becoming increasingly sophisticated, offering new opportunities to understand how metabolic regulation influences brain function,” states senior author Christoffer Clemmensen, research group leader at the University of Copenhagen. “By disentangling the direct and indirect effects of these drugs on the brain, we may ultimately be able to develop treatments specifically aimed at preserving cognitive function.”

The authors argue that the lack of efficacy in established Alzheimer’s disease should not preclude investigating cognitive protection in metabolic cohorts.

Individuals with obesity and type 2 diabetes frequently exhibit subclinical executive deficits—including reduced processing speed, difficulties with complex working memory, and blunted cognitive flexibility. These performance drops stem directly from metabolic and vascular strain. Introducing next-generation incretin poly-agonists during early metabolic dysregulation could prevent these microvascular insults from compounding into later dementia.

The researchers urge the scientific community to integrate comprehensive neurocognitive assessments from day one in ongoing and future obesity pharmacotherapy trials. Concurrently, they highlight the necessity of careful monitoring for sarcopenia: in frail or elderly individuals, precipitous reductions in skeletal muscle mass could impair physical stability and compromise overall quality of life, counteracting neurological gains.

Did GLP-1 drugs like semaglutide slow down Alzheimer’s disease in clinical trials?

No. In Novo Nordisk’s phase 3 EVOKE and EVOKE+ clinical trials, semaglutide did not slow cognitive decline or disease progression in patients with early Alzheimer’s disease, despite altering several biological disease markers.

How might obesity drugs still protect brain function?

Obesity and type 2 diabetes cause microvascular injury, insulin resistance, and inflammation that damage executive function and processing speed. When administered early, before neurodegeneration is established, these drugs may protect the brain by improving systemic metabolic health and directly activating neuroprotective GLP-1 receptors in brain tissue.

What precautions do the authors highlight for older patients taking these drugs?

The researchers emphasize the risk of lean muscle mass loss and sarcopenia. In older or frail individuals, rapid weight loss could diminish functional strength, increase fall risks, and undermine the overall clinical benefits of the therapy.

Pharmacotherapies for obesity and type 2 diabetes are increasingly being evaluated for their effects on cognition and brain health.

Evidence from preclinical models, observational cohorts, and clinical trials has intensified interest, but findings remain mixed. A central challenge is to determine whether potential cognitive effects arise from direct actions on neural circuits, indirect consequences of improved cardiometabolic health, or both.

This Review examines the evidence across cardiometabolic disease, aging, and Alzheimer’s disease, focusing on glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1)–based therapies and combination approaches that engage glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide, glucagon, and amylin pathways.

We assess how receptor pharmacology, brain exposure, metabolic context, and disease stage shape cognitive outcomes and discuss how mechanistic clarity could guide therapies aimed at preserving or improving cognition.
